# Reproducible transcription is not preservation of uncertainty

## Abstract

An audit of three book-export packages found that their readable Markdown could be reconstructed byte for byte while omitting every one of 153 explicitly recorded uncertainty fields attached to generated illustration descriptions. The underlying structured records remained intact. A separate derivative projection preserved all 153 fields and labeled all 917 descriptions as generated and unreviewed; removing only the recorded insertions restored the original readable bytes. This is a completed finite demonstration of a representation defect and its local correction. It does not measure OCR accuracy, validate the descriptions, or establish a benefit of graph storage over a serializer implementing the same rule. This report separates the corpus observations, later export integration, unresolved trace defects, and a small public synthetic illustration of the projection rule.

## Question and source scope

The question is whether a transformation delivers the qualifications attached to the content a reader actually receives. Preserving the source archive is necessary for audit, but it does not make an omitted qualification visible in a derived document. The scientific distinction is developed in [Semantic objects and context](../../../papers/semantic-objects-and-context/PAPER.md) and [Constrained transformations](../../../papers/constrained-transformations/PAPER.md).

The studied corpus covered cooking, marine navigation, and electrical automation. The retained source audit identifies a 1952 edition of *Книга о вкусной и здоровой пище*, G. I. Fain's 1977 *Навигация, лоция и мореходная астрономия*, and V. A. Timofeev's 1964 *Сто схемных задач по автоматике*. Dates and page counts describe the observed editions. They do not establish present applicability of advice or authorize practical use.

| Export family | Page anchors | Imported generated descriptions | Nonempty uncertainty fields |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| Cooking | 456 | 374 | 111 |
| Navigation | 289 | 319 | 16 |
| Electrical automation | 154 | 224 | 26 |
| Total | 899 | 917 | 153 |

The [structural corpus report](evidence/structural-corpus-report.json) supports the counts. Pages, descriptions, and uncertainty fields are different units. They are not 1,969 independently verified observations, and a page anchor is not an expert judgment on a page.

The structural builder and its validator shared a lexical table detector. Agreement on its table counts is therefore not independent validation of table recognition. The report also records zero image-byte checks in this structural pass; consistency of declared image hashes is a metadata observation.

## Audit and transformation method

The [corpus audit](evidence/corpus-audit-source.json) examined 39 files, thirteen per export. For 33, the export receipt contained expected sizes and hashes, all of which matched. The remaining README and receipt files were pinned by the observer; no receipt was treated as authenticating its own bytes.

The observer reconstructed the readable output from the source document, editing ledger, and structured illustration records. The target readable text was used for comparison rather than inserted into the generated output. The reconstruction covered 174 recorded edits, seven italic-wrapper mappings in the navigation export, three caption-to-image-alt copies, and the generated descriptions. All three readable files matched the retained targets byte for byte.

The mapping was developed after inspecting the available examples. It is a known-data reconstruction, not a blind qualification of the producer. Every replayed editing operation retained its unreviewed status. Reversibility established what the operation did to the representation; it did not establish that the editorial change was linguistically correct.

The important counterexample appeared in the illustration projection. The readable template emitted description, purpose where present, and recognized image text. It did not emit the separately stored `uncertain` field. All 153 nonempty values were absent from the corresponding readable descriptions. This finding concerns that exact field and projection. It does not assert that every description lacked all cautious wording, or that the JSON source had been destroyed.

## Preserving uncertainty as a testable obligation

For a record \(r\) with an explicit qualification \(u(r)\), the relevant requirement is that the delivered projection contain the qualification, or explicitly declare the permitted loss. Byte identity of a different object does not satisfy that requirement. A projection can be perfectly reproducible and consistently omit the same field.

The corrective derivative therefore introduced a narrow rule. After each generated description, it inserted the declared generated/unreviewed status. When `uncertain` was nonempty, it inserted the exact field value with an explicit label. Every insertion carried an input row, record hash, asset identity, anchor, and output range. The source text and original readable files were preserved.

The transformation profile and expected counts were fixed before generation, after the audit had revealed the defect. The [derivative report](evidence/uncertainty-projection-source.json) records the outcome:

| Obligation | Observed result | Boundary |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Preserve generated/unreviewed status | 917/917 descriptions labeled | The labels do not independently assess the description |
| Preserve each nonempty uncertainty value | 153/153 exact values delivered | These are unreviewed model statements, not calibrated probabilities |
| Preserve the previous readable representation | Removing only declared insertions restores all three original files | The experiment concerns this specified transformation |
| Keep source inputs unchanged | All 39 retained source-file hashes unchanged | Integrity is distinct from source correctness |

No new OCR, subject model call, or human domain review occurred in this transformation. Imported model descriptions were already present in the exports. The zero new-call count must not be read as a claim that the corpus contains no model-produced material.

## Domain-specific consequences

The three domains exposed different dependencies. In a cooking instruction, a qualification can belong to one ingredient branch or a later serving operation. Merging branches or moving an action can alter the procedure even if familiar words survive. In navigation, a table or diagram can depend on a legend, a sign convention, a preceding page, and applicability to a particular instrument. Exact numerical transcription alone does not deliver those conditions. In an electrical diagram, visible connections can support a qualitative description while numerical outputs remain unknown without input values and operating context.

These examples motivated engineering obligations; they are not new cooking, navigation, or circuit-design recommendations. The later [projection study](../executable-projections-and-revision/STUDY.md) evaluates selected tasks involving such dependencies. Neither study treats a historical book's age as an automatic proof of error, or as an adequate reason to authorize contemporary action.

## Trace defects and later integration

The audit also found unresolved addressability. Of 5,538 quality flags, the observed mapping addressed 5,518; sixteen table or formula offsets and four orphan flags remained unresolved. A disappearing flag was not counted as repaired OCR. It could reflect changed text, changed diagnostics, or missing provenance.

A separate raw-to-document analysis reproduced all recorded correction rows in the cooking and automation exports. For navigation, 53 of 58 rows were reproduced, leaving five rows in two blocks without a unique raw-serialization binding. Those rows were not labeled five OCR errors. The evidence was insufficient to reconstruct the producer's transformation, which is a different finding.

The twenty unresolved quality flags plus these five correction rows are the 25 unresolved coordinate traces named in the later integration summary. This reconciles its inventory; it does not turn two different kinds of trace defect into a single OCR error rate.

Later local integration processed the same 917 description records in two runs. Each recorded 19,305 satisfied field checks and no violated field checks, preserving 153 nonempty and 764 empty uncertainty values. The [integration summary](evidence/local-export-integration-report.json) also preserves the initial control result of fifteen expected outcomes out of sixteen. One authored control supplied pretty JSON where JSONL was required, causing an earlier-than-intended rejection. Its separately retained correction exercised the intended missing-uncertainty case. A post-replacement control distinguished visibility of a validated new snapshot from unconfirmed durability.

This integration occurred after export. It was not installed in the upstream OCR producer. Physical power-loss durability was not tested. Seven truncated descriptions formed a targeted review queue; the other nonempty uncertainty fields were not automatically equated with defective or truncated outputs.

## Public illustration and retained evidence

The source books, OCR transcriptions, extracted illustrations, and full generated book descriptions are not redistributed here. Project-authored reports and aggregate metadata are provided under readable filenames, with exact JSON identities and separate rendered-prose provenance in [origin.json](evidence/origin.json). Full replay of the original corpus audit requires the specified source packages and original runner closure; the reports alone do not supply them.

A [corrected public illustration](checks/demonstrate_uncertainty_projection.py) uses the four invented records from the preserved edition-1.0 program, two with nonempty uncertainty. It compares the base serializer against a [separately stored literal target](checks/synthetic-base.txt), rather than comparing an expression with itself. A changed-description mutation fails that comparison. The projection preserves both fields, marks all four records, records insertion ranges and rejects an absent uncertainty field; removing exactly those insertions restores the stored target, while damaging an insertion range breaks restoration. These are finite checks of the invented projection rule, not a reconstruction of the historical source-plus-editing-ledger pipeline. Run:

```bash
python3 checks/demonstrate_uncertainty_projection.py
```

The [saved corrected demonstration](checks/uncertainty-projection-check.json) is a new synthetic illustration. The [earlier program](evidence/replay_uncertainty_projection.py) and [earlier result](evidence/public-projection-demonstration.json) are retained unchanged; their `base_reconstruction_exact` predicate compared the same construction with itself and supplied no independent reconstruction evidence. The corrected harness removes that inference. Its four records are not substituted for the 917 historical descriptions, and its checks are not added to historical corpus totals. The [correction record](../review-corrections.json) binds the new fixture, code and result separately.

The corpus audit and derivative report are Russian prose views. The methods, missing-field finding, coordinate counts and qualification limits above are English summaries of their relevant passages; the raw sources have not been translated or rewritten in place. The [evidence language index](../evidence-language-index.json) records the language of every file.

## Limits and conclusion

The corpus's character error rate (CER) and word error rate (WER) remained unmeasured. With a declared alignment and reference unitization, each counts substitutions, deletions and insertions divided by reference characters or words; the historical audit supplied neither a complete ground-truth transcript nor that measurement protocol. `null` therefore does not mean zero error. The [evaluation-method note](../../../reference/bibliography/BIBLIOGRAPHY.md#evaluation-methods) identifies the scoring background.

Image checks belong to different stages and denominators. The earlier reader source reports three selected PNGs checked out of 918 described images. The later 917-description corpus/structural audit checked no image bytes. A separate addressability experiment checked three selected JPEGs, as its [retained source](../executable-projections-and-revision/evidence/addressability-study-source.json) states. None is a byte audit of the whole image collection, and the earlier three-of-918 observation cannot be reassigned to the later 917-record pass. Neither every OCR passage nor the calibration of model uncertainty was assessed. Structural graph construction created addressable source structure; it did not thereby extract a verified semantic knowledge graph.

The established engineering result is precise: the chosen readable template lost an explicitly represented qualification field; a declared and reversible projection preserved it on the inspected finite corpus. A simple serializer can implement that rule. The evidence supports an explicit preservation obligation and supplies a concrete counterexample to equating hashes with meaning preservation. It does not establish superiority of the larger architecture. Related terms are defined in the [glossary](../../../reference/glossary/GLOSSARY.md).

BLOB papers/constrained-transformations/PAPER.md b9ec18c1ee93c869bd31a0e097014338605ba2937979db827bf8cde5f63d4050 original 16395

title: "Constrained Transformations and the Limits of Knowledge Transfer"
artifact: "scientific-paper"

author: "Pavel Valentov"
language: "en"

description: "Conditional preservation, composition and reconstruction of knowledge under explicit observations and losses."

# Constrained Transformations and the Limits of Knowledge Transfer

**Author:** Pavel Valentov · **Publication edition:** 1.1

## Abstract

Knowledge is repeatedly transformed: interpreted from a source, normalized, summarized, projected into a task, or instantiated for a new situation. A successful transformation need not preserve every property, but it must preserve the properties on which its permitted use depends. This paper models transformations as partial relations with explicit preconditions, protected observations, postconditions, and loss records. We prove conditional composition and preservation results, distinguish observation equivalence from identity, and show when a projection can or cannot be reversed. A separate result explains the limits of transferring a rule between domains. The claims concern declared transition models and successful executions; they do not imply that a transformation terminates, that its semantic annotations are complete, or that a language model follows the specified relation. The resulting obligations provide a precise basis for executable projections and for testing transformations with counterexamples.

**Keywords:** transformation contract; partial relation; semantic preservation; projection; transfer; reconstruction.

## 1. Research question and relation to prior work

An answer may retain the main words of a source while losing a condition that makes the answer usable. Conversely, a change of notation can preserve everything required for a particular task. The question is therefore not whether a transformation changes its input, but **which observations must remain valid, under which assumptions, for which use**.

We develop a task-relative account of transformation. It applies to changes of representation and to restricted inference, while keeping them distinct. Converting a value to another unit, summarizing an argument, and deriving a consequence require different premises. Naming all three “processing” does not supply a common correctness guarantee.

Hoare's account of program correctness separates preconditions, program execution, and postconditions [1]. Our formulation uses the same discipline at the boundary of knowledge operations. It does not claim that arbitrary natural-language transformations already have a mechanically verified semantics. The contribution is to expose the proof obligations and the cases in which a claim of preservation is too strong.

## 2. A transformation model

Let \(X\) and \(Y\) be declared spaces of input and output states. A transformation is a relation

\[
T\subseteq X\times Y.
\]

A relation, rather than a total function, permits multiple valid outputs and inputs on which the operation has no admitted result. A transformation contract specifies an input predicate \(P\), output predicate \(Q\), protected observations, and an account of allowed losses. Its partial-correctness condition is

\[
\forall x,y:\ P(x)\land T(x,y)\Rightarrow Q(y).
\]

This implication concerns successful transitions in \(T\). It neither promises an output for every admitted input nor bounds computation time. A practical implementation must separately distinguish successful output, refusal, ambiguity, insufficient budget, and execution failure.

A **protected observation** consists of interpretation functions \(p_X:X\to O\) and \(p_Y:Y\to O\) into a common observation space. Preservation means

\[
P(x)\land T(x,y)\Rightarrow p_X(x)=p_Y(y).
\]

The observations are selected for the task. They may include a numerical value, a referent, a condition, an attribution, or an unresolved uncertainty. Equality of two unknown observations preserves the recorded uncertainty; it does not establish the unknown proposition.

Not all transformations require equality. A projection may retain only a declared subset of observations; an inference may establish an output proposition from input premises under a rule. The contract must state which relation is required. Calling every relation “equivalence” would erase the distinction between preserving information and deriving a consequence.

## 3. Composition and preservation

### Proposition 1. Compatible partial-correctness contracts compose

Suppose \(T_1\subseteq X\times Y\) satisfies \(\{P\}T_1\{Q\}\), and \(T_2\subseteq Y\times Z\) satisfies \(\{R\}T_2\{S\}\). If \(Q(y)\Rightarrow R(y)\) for every intermediate state, then their relational composition satisfies \(\{P\}(T_2\circ T_1)\{S\}\).

**Proof.** For an admitted composed transition \((x,z)\), there is a state \(y\) such that \(T_1(x,y)\) and \(T_2(y,z)\). From \(P(x)\), the first contract yields \(Q(y)\). The compatibility assumption yields \(R(y)\), and the second contract yields \(S(z)\). ∎

This proposition does not prove total correctness. A second operation may have no successful transition for an intermediate state even when its partial-correctness implication holds. Existence of a result, termination, and resource sufficiency require additional assumptions.

### Proposition 2. A shared protected observation survives a finite chain

Let \(n\geq1\) and \(T_i\subseteq X_{i-1}\times X_i\) for \(1\leq i\leq n\). Each step satisfies \(\{P_i\}T_i\{Q_i\}\) and preserves observations \(p_i^-:X_{i-1}\to O\), \(p_i^+:X_i\to O\) whenever \(P_i\) holds. At every junction require

\[
Q_i(x)\Rightarrow P_{i+1}(x),
\qquad
Q_i(x)\Rightarrow p_i^+(x)=p_{i+1}^-(x),
\quad 1\leq i<n.
\]

For any successful chain \(T_i(x_{i-1},x_i)\) beginning with \(P_1(x_0)\), these conditions imply

\[
p_1^-(x_0)=p_n^+(x_n).
\]

**Proof.** Partial correctness of the first step yields \(Q_1(x_1)\). The first junction implication supplies \(P_2(x_1)\); induction gives every subsequent precondition and postcondition. Each step therefore preserves its observation on the actual transition. The second junction implication equates the preceding output observation with the following input observation on each intermediate state. Substituting those within-step and between-step equalities gives the displayed endpoint equality. ∎

Both junction requirements are necessary to this argument. On \(\{0,1\}\), take two identity transitions, with identity observations at both interfaces of the first step and Boolean negation at both interfaces of the second. Each step preserves its own observations, but the chain starts with \(x\) and ends with \(1-x\); its observation junction fails. Separately, let the first transition be identity with an unrestricted precondition and the second map both inputs to \(1\), with precondition \(x=1\). With identity observations throughout, both local contracts preserve observations on their admitted inputs. Nevertheless the composed path \(0\to0\to1\) changes the observation because the second precondition is not met.

The common-observation requirement is substantive. If one operation preserves a numerical value but drops its interpretation, and another preserves only the remaining text, the chain has not preserved a quantity with units. Local declarations about different properties do not combine into a stronger end-to-end claim.

A second counterexample concerns recipients. An operation may omit a condition because its current recipient already knows it. Passing the result to a different recipient changes that precondition. A technically intact record of the earlier check cannot restore a condition that the new recipient lacks.

## 4. Equivalence, projection, and residual information

For a fixed observation function \(p\), define

\[
x\sim_p x'\iff p(x)=p(x').
\]

This is an equivalence relation because equality is reflexive, symmetric, and transitive. It is an equivalence of declared observations, not of object identity or all meaning. Enlarging the observation set can split an equivalence class that was adequate for a narrower task.

A transformation descends to equivalence classes only if it respects the chosen relation. For a deterministic transformation \(f\), this requires that \(x\sim_p x'\) imply \(f(x)\sim_{p'}f(x')\). For a partial transformation, its domain must also contain every equivalent representative or none of them. Otherwise admission or output depends on which representative was selected, and replacing one representative by another is not justified.

A **projection** \(\pi_J\) retains observations indexed by \(J\). For coordinate projections, repeated application is idempotent and projection to a smaller set loses no further information about those retained coordinates. These properties follow from the defined operation; they must not be assumed for an arbitrary summarizer merely called a projection.

### Proposition 3. A noninjective transformation cannot have a left inverse

Let \(f:X\to Y\) map two distinct inputs \(x_1\ne x_2\) to the same output. There is no function \(g:Y\to X\) satisfying \(g(f(x))=x\) for all \(x\in X\).

**Proof.** Such a function would require the same value \(g(f(x_1))=g(f(x_2))\) to equal both distinct inputs. ∎

Reconstruction becomes possible if a retained residual \(\rho(x)\) makes the pair \((f(x),\rho(x))\) sufficiently informative and a reconstruction function is defined on that pair. For example, a projection of a two-component record can be reversed when the omitted component is stored as residual information. That restoration uses additional retained information. It is not evidence that compression creates information or that every summary is reversible.

Bancilhon and Spyratos use a view together with a complement to determine the database state and constrain view-update translation [3]. That established construction motivates the distinction between a projection and the information needed to reconstruct its source. The present model states a reconstruction obligation for knowledge artifacts; it does not establish the view-update laws or a general bidirectional transformation for natural-language summaries.

Loss records must therefore distinguish intentionally omitted content, unresolved interpretation, and information retained elsewhere. A link to a residual helps only when the recipient can retrieve and interpret it within the permitted scope and resource budget.

A finite Boolean model gives an exact special case of shared content and residual information. Write \(M(F)\) for the models of formula \(F\). Entailment is inclusion of model sets. The formula \(C=F\lor G\) is a strongest common consequence of \(F\) and \(G\): both entail \(C\), and any consequence of both is true throughout \(M(F)\cup M(G)=M(C)\). Define \(R_F=\neg C\lor F\). Then

\[
C\land R_F=C\land(\neg C\lor F)\equiv C\land F\equiv F,
\]

because \(F\) entails \(C\). This proves reconstruction with that residual in the declared logic. It does not establish economy: \(R_F=F\) also reconstructs \(F\), and a claim that another residual is smaller requires a separate size measure and comparison. Nor does the construction identify a useful common theme between arbitrary documents. The residual convention is not unique outside \(C\). An inconsistent premise set has no models and classically entails every formula; operational admission must therefore separately require an appropriate consistent, nonempty interpretation rather than turn vacuous entailment into a recommendation.

## 5. Transfer between tasks and domains

Transfer is a directed operation. A rule applicable under conditions \(C\) can be used for a target only after those conditions have been mapped and checked. Similar vocabulary is a candidate-generation signal, not a proof that the required relation is preserved.

Consider a source rule

\[
C(x)\Rightarrow P(x).
\]

To infer \(P'(y)\) in a target representation, one needs a mapping between the relevant source and target objects, justification of the corresponding condition, and a bridge showing that the source conclusion supports \(P'\). Without such a bridge, the source rule remains a statement about its original interpretation.

An abstract counterexample is sufficient. Two systems can share a scalar variable called “size,” while one has output proportional to size and the other has output proportional to its square. Matching the variable name and one observed point does not justify transferring the linear rule. A usable transfer contract identifies the governing relation and its domain, rather than relying on surface similarity.

Units provide a limited positive case. If two units describe the same dimension and their relation is an exact rational factor, exact conversion preserves the represented quantity. The conclusion depends on dimension compatibility, the factor, and exact arithmetic. It does not validate an incorrectly extracted input value, an uncertain measurement, or an unrelated domain equation.

## 6. Records, implementation, and tests

A transformation record should identify the input and output revisions, declared operation, active preconditions, protected observations, verification result, and any loss or residual. Provenance is useful here because it makes dependencies inspectable [2]. Provenance alone does not establish that the operation satisfied its contract.

An implementation claiming compliance must connect its actual outputs to the relation used in the argument. A contract written next to a model does not constrain the model's behavior unless execution is checked or the implementation is independently justified. A finite checker can establish only properties in its supported language and bounded domain. Unsupported rules remain unsupported; they do not become valid because the system produces a plausible explanation.

Tests should include incompatible intermediate types, a dropped exception, changed recipients, a noninjective projection offered as reversible, an omitted residual, and a transfer with matching labels but incompatible relations. Positive tests should vary irrelevant features while keeping protected observations fixed. Negative tests should change one material assumption at a time and require refusal, qualified output, or a failed check.

The propositions establish the selected composition, preservation, and reconstruction properties under their explicit assumptions. They are not a completeness claim about all knowledge transformations. No empirical claim is made that a particular extractor or generator reliably supplies those assumptions. The corresponding measurement question is the frequency and consequence of violations on independently constructed transformations, with separate reporting for interpretation, execution, and verification errors.

## 7. Connections to the published engineering work

The [executable projections and revision study](../../engineering/studies/executable-projections-and-revision/STUDY.md) reports finite consumer checks for partial extraction and addressed revision. The [dependency closure and package delivery study](../../engineering/studies/dependency-closure-and-package-delivery/STUDY.md) reports a fixed dependency graph, preserved artifact payloads, and a historical preliminary delivery decision. These controls concern the stated packages and projections; neither study evaluates every transformation relation or cross-domain transfer proposed here.

The shared [glossary](../../reference/glossary/GLOSSARY.md) defines project terminology. [Semantic objects and context](../semantic-objects-and-context/PAPER.md) supplies the interpretation boundary; [controlled knowledge reuse](../controlled-knowledge-reuse/PAPER.md) applies the preservation argument to previously prepared artifacts.

## References

1. Hoare, C. A. R. “An Axiomatic Basis for Computer Programming.” *Communications of the ACM* 12(10), 1969, 576–580, 583. <https://doi.org/10.1145/363235.363259>
2. W3C. *PROV-DM: The PROV Data Model*. W3C Recommendation, 2013. <https://www.w3.org/TR/prov-dm/>
3. Bancilhon, F., and Spyratos, N. “Update Semantics of Relational Views.” *ACM Transactions on Database Systems* 6(4), 1981, 557–575. [Original article](https://cs.uwaterloo.ca/~david/cs848s18/constant-complement.pdf).

# Knowledge Contracts and the Admission of Prepared Knowledge

**Author:** Pavel Valentov · **Publication edition:** 1.1

## Abstract

Preparing knowledge in advance can reduce repeated work, but a previously acceptable artifact is not automatically acceptable for a new task. This paper defines a knowledge contract as an explicit account of requirements, constraints, grounds, permitted transformations, and verification obligations. It distinguishes that contract from its professional specializations and from a prepared artifact produced under it. Reuse is modeled as a directed admission relation, evaluated for a particular request and state. We prove a conditional preservation result requiring sound prior verification, complete material dependencies, agreement on relevant request components, coverage of the new requirements, and preservation by every permitted transformation output. Counterexamples cover changed requests, permissions, incomplete dependencies, negative search results, and races between admission and delivery. The contribution is a precise boundary between reusable preparation and current authorization, including cases where rebuilding or rejection is required. The argument does not establish automatic discovery of every semantic dependency, industrial coordination guarantees, or a universal economic advantage from caching.

**Keywords:** knowledge contract; compilation; reuse admission; constraints; dependencies; current authorization.

## 1. Research question and relation to caching

A system may prepare a summary, context package, workflow, or professional instruction once and use it repeatedly. Repetition saves work only if the prepared result remains suitable. Identical request text can hide a changed recipient, policy, source revision, or time interpretation.

We ask: **when does an earlier verification justify a new use of a prepared artifact?** The problem resembles caching, but the cached object carries semantic and normative obligations as well as bytes. HTTP caching provides explicit rules for freshness and reuse [1], and extensions describe limited stale-serving behavior [2]. Those protocols are relevant comparisons; their guarantees do not automatically apply to knowledge interpretation or authorization.

The contribution is a contract model that makes the extra admission obligations explicit. It treats reuse as a decision under conditions rather than a consequence of similarity, popularity, or storage location. Incremental build correctness already requires consistency between a result and its complete task dependencies [3]. The theorem below uses the same dependence discipline while separately requiring coverage of a new knowledge request and current authority; those obligations are not established by a successful build alone.

## 2. Contracts and their professional specialization

A **knowledge contract** specifies a bounded use: its objective, requirements, mandatory constraints, admissible grounds, permitted transformations, expected result, and verification or stopping conditions. Its content can be represented in different formats. The scientific concept does not depend on one schema version or transport envelope.

Constraints are first-class obligations. They can limit scope, resources, time, recipient, authority, interpretation, or allowed operation. A preference can guide selection among admissible options. A mandatory constraint cannot be compensated by a high utility score. If a mandatory condition is unknown, the corresponding admission has not passed.

The contract does not itself establish truth, grant authority, or supply a missing capability. It states what must be supported and checked. A structurally valid contract can still contain a false premise or an unimplementable demand.

In a professional profile, a **role** specifies expected responsibilities; a **competency** specifies an ability under stated conditions, while a competency assessment binds supporting evidence to a particular executor; a **skill** is a reusable procedure with conditions, steps, and checks; and a **blueprint** organizes roles, procedures, and decision points for a class of work. These are the project's operational definitions, not a claim of one universal external taxonomy.

A **Work Item** instantiates an objective with actual requirements and outputs. A **capability** concerns an available operation. **Authorization** concerns permission to perform it in the current context. A role can be responsible for an outcome without possessing every capability or permission needed to produce it. A skill description does not establish that an executor meets a competency requirement, and a successful attempt does not establish that ability across every task in the described class.

## 3. Preparation and the reuse relation

Let \(q\) include the task, requirements, referents, recipient, isolation scope, resource budget, and time interpretation. Let \(S\) be a pinned source state and \(b\) a preparation recipe. A preparation attempt produces, when successful,

\[
\operatorname{Compile}(b,q,S)\to(a,D,E,L),
\]

where \(a\) is an artifact revision, \(D\) its declared dependencies, \(E\) its verification evidence, and \(L\) its coverage, losses, and unresolved conditions. Preparation can fail or produce an explicitly partial result. A content hash checks bytes under a declared serialization; it does not establish truth or deterministic regeneration.

For a new request \(q'\), policy \(p\), and current state \(s\), write

\[
a\rightsquigarrow_{p,s}q'
\]

when the artifact is admitted for that use. This relation is directed. A complete artifact may support an authorized narrower projection; a partial artifact does not support a broader task merely because the same topic is mentioned.

Possible decisions include reuse unchanged, instantiate with checking, revalidate, rebuild, bypass reuse, and reject the requested use. Revalidation is an outstanding obligation, not permission to deliver before the check. An instantiated result has its own content identity and actual-output checks.

Build dependencies and admission dependencies differ. A generator version can matter for provenance or a known defect; current permission can matter at every delivery. Neither invalidating everything after every tool update nor ignoring all interpretation changes is a justified general policy.

## 4. Conditional preservation theorem

Fix an admission policy \(p\). Let \(P_1(x,q,s),\ldots,P_m(x,q,s)\) be properties of an artifact revision \(x\), request \(q\), and state \(s\), interpreted under fixed semantics. For each property, let \(\delta_j(s)\) be its relevant state observation and \(\kappa_j(q)\) its relevant request observation. Let the complete mandatory requirements of the new request be partitioned as

\[
R(q')=R_{\mathrm{artifact}}(q')\cup R_{\mathrm{use}}(q').
\]

The first group concerns the artifact properties justified by preparation; the second contains independent conditions to be discharged at current use, including authorization and delivery. Requirement discharge has the [applicability paper's meaning](../semantic-objects-and-context/PAPER.md#3-context-and-applicability): a passing assessment or a justified exemption for the exact subject and scope, never an unsupported or unknown condition. Assume:

1. **Sound prior verification.** \(P_j(a,q,s_0)\) actually holds for every \(j\). The presence of a positive receipt is not itself this premise.
2. **Complete and unchanged observations.** The manifest \(D\) contains every state input material to the selected properties and their interpretation. For every relevant \(x,q,s\), each property factors as \(P_j(x,q,s)=\widehat P_j(x,\kappa_j(q),\delta_j(s))\) for a fixed predicate \(\widehat P_j\). Moreover \(\delta_j(s_0)=\delta_j(s_1)\) and \(\kappa_j(q)=\kappa_j(q')\). This expresses completeness for request components as well as state components.
3. **Preservation for every permitted transformation output.** The permitted transformation is a relation \(T\), possibly the identity. For every \(a'\) with \(T(a,a')\), and every \(j\), \(P_j(a,q',s_1)\Rightarrow P_j(a',q',s_1)\).
4. **Coverage of the new artifact requirements.** Under the fixed assessment semantics, the selected properties suffice to discharge every requirement in \(R_{\mathrm{artifact}}(q')\). Explicitly, for every relevant output \(x\),

   \[
   \bigwedge_{j=1}^{m} P_j(x,q',s_1)
   \Rightarrow
   \bigwedge_{r\in R_{\mathrm{artifact}}(q')}
   \operatorname{Discharged}_r(x,q',s_1).
   \]

5. **Current independent admission.** For every proposed output \(a'\) with \(T(a,a')\), every requirement in \(R_{\mathrm{use}}(q')\) is discharged for that output at the declared current admission point. A check for another recipient, representation, or generation does not satisfy this premise.

Then every such output \(a'\) retains all selected properties for \((q',s_1)\), and all requirements in \(R(q')\) are discharged. It is therefore admitted under the specified policy and scope.

**Proof.** By assumptions 1–2, for each \(j\),

\[
P_j(a,q,s_0)
=\widehat P_j(a,\kappa_j(q),\delta_j(s_0))
=\widehat P_j(a,\kappa_j(q'),\delta_j(s_1))
=P_j(a,q',s_1).
\]

Thus \(P_j(a,q',s_1)\) holds. Assumption 3 transfers it to every permitted transformation output \(a'\). Assumption 4 discharges the new artifact requirements, and assumption 5 discharges the independent current-use requirements. Their union is the complete declared requirement set, so no mandatory requirement remains outside the argument. ∎

Two counterexamples explain why unchanged state alone is insufficient. An artifact can establish Boolean condition \(A\) while a new request also requires \(B\), which is false; preserving \(A\) does not cover \(B\). Separately, the property “the artifact's referent equals the requested referent” can hold for \(q\) and fail for \(q'\) without any source-state change. The first case violates requirement coverage; the second violates request-observation agreement.

The theorem is deliberately conditional. A manifest labeled “complete” does not prove its completeness, a checker can be unsound, and an unjustified coverage implication cannot authorize a new task. The result quantifies over successful permitted outputs; it does not promise that \(T\) has any output or terminates. Natural-language interpretation, hidden dependencies, and current authority need their own justification. Preservation also cannot make a false premise true: it can maintain a faithful record of a mistaken source. For a genuinely narrower requirement set with unchanged interpretations and assessments, [mandatory strengthening](../semantic-objects-and-context/PAPER.md#proposition-3-mandatory-strengthening-narrows-certified-applicability) supplies the corresponding subset implication; a change of request is not automatically such a narrowing.

## 5. Dependencies and scope counterexamples

Dependencies can concern content fields, relations, alternative grounds, interpretation rules, normative bases, representations, and search scope. Fine granularity can avoid unnecessary rebuilding only when it preserves completeness for the selected properties.

Negative, exhaustive, counted, and ranked answers expose a common failure. Suppose an artifact states that no record satisfies a predicate. Recording only previously returned record identifiers is insufficient: a new matching record changes the answer without modifying any earlier result. The relevant dependency includes the searched scope, visibility, predicate, and any completeness assumptions. Exact top-ranked results additionally depend on ranking and tie rules. Approximate search does not become exhaustive because its output is cached.

Multimodal dependencies can concern an image revision, region, legend, or transformation. Correcting a symbol can invalidate an unchanged textual interpretation. Moving identical bytes may require a locator or delivery check without changing semantic content. These cases require different responses.

Alternative grounds also matter. Losing one source need not invalidate a conclusion if an independently sufficient permitted ground remains. A dependency traversal can identify affected artifacts, but deciding whether they remain supported requires the declared support rule and current assessments.

## 6. State, time, and delivery

Lifecycle, residency, freshness, and reuse eligibility are separate dimensions. An artifact can be ready and resident but unauthorized for a particular recipient. Eviction can change latency without changing its content. A recent cache hit is evidence of use, not of renewed validation.

Time policies must be explicit. **Strict current use** requires the relevant current conditions at a declared admission point. **Historical pinned use** preserves a past state for the requested purpose while still respecting present access and revocation conditions. **Bounded stale use** permits specified age or divergence only where the contract allows it. Known revocation, corruption, or incompatibility is not repaired by an unexpired time-to-live.

Checking immediately before delivery is insufficient for a strong current-state guarantee if a material condition can change between the check and the protected operation. For example, a permission can pass, then be withdrawn, then the artifact can be sent. Every individual step can be implemented as described while the desired current-admission claim fails.

A stronger guarantee requires a declared admission point and a coordination mechanism that binds the relevant evaluation to that point: a coherent snapshot, suitable lock, valid lease, or equivalent protocol under stated assumptions. The guarantee concerns that point and scope; it does not promise that the world will remain unchanged afterward.

Publication has a related race. A slow preparation attempt must not replace a newer artifact merely because it finishes later. Generation checks and protected publication can prevent that overwrite under their storage assumptions. Repeated or reordered invalidation events can be handled idempotently, but lost events require reconciliation or independent validation. Event deduplication alone does not recover missing information.

## 7. Economic and practical limits

Admission first determines which routes are permitted. Cost or predicted utility can then rank those routes. A cheap inadmissible result is not a valid optimization candidate. Reuse may reduce repeated preparation while adding storage, validation, invalidation, reconstruction, and coordination costs.

Comparisons should include a valid cold path and account for the same required quality and authority conditions. Provider-level caching, retrieval caching, prepared context, and professional artifacts are different units of reuse. Savings attributed to the same avoided operation must not be counted repeatedly across layers.

The present result is the conditional admission argument and its counterexamples. It establishes no universal speedup, cost reduction, or production concurrency guarantee. Those are workload- and implementation-specific claims that require their own measurements and failure models. Repeated successful reuse does not by itself raise the truth or completeness status of the underlying knowledge.

## 8. Connections to the published engineering work

The [reuse admission and cost controls study](../../engineering/studies/reuse-admission-and-cost-controls/STUDY.md) examines finite admission decisions and accounting controls. The [contract profile conformance study](../../engineering/studies/contract-profile-conformance/STUDY.md) addresses supported representations. The [dependency closure and package delivery study](../../engineering/studies/dependency-closure-and-package-delivery/STUDY.md) addresses closure and actual delivery.

Machine interfaces and their compatibility versions belong in the [engineering core specification](../../engineering/specifications/knowledge-contract-core/SPECIFICATION.md). The shared [glossary](../../reference/glossary/GLOSSARY.md), [constrained transformations](../constrained-transformations/PAPER.md), and [knowledge utility](../knowledge-utility/PAPER.md) complete the conceptual links.

## References

2. Nottingham, M. *HTTP Cache-Control Extensions for Stale Content*. RFC 5861, 2010. <https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5861>
3. Mokhov, A., Mitchell, N., and Peyton Jones, S. “Build Systems à la Carte.” *Proceedings of the ACM on Programming Languages* 2(ICFP), article 79, 2018, 1–29. [Author's copy](https://simon.peytonjones.org/assets/pdfs/build-systems-original.pdf). <https://doi.org/10.1145/3236774>

# Evidence, Reasoning, and Falsification in Knowledge Systems

**Author:** Pavel Valentov · **Publication edition:** 1.1

## Abstract

A knowledge system can pass structural checks while offering an unsupported conclusion. It can also possess a sound formal argument whose assumptions are not satisfied by an implementation. This paper specifies how to connect claims, grounds, inference rules, checks, and decisions without confusing their evidential roles. We define a claim record and a finite justification graph, prove a conditional soundness property for supported acyclic derivations, and characterize the scope of finite testing. We then give a falsification protocol that separates counterexamples to a model, implementation defects, annotation failures, and limits of empirical transfer. Evaluation must use pinned requirements, explicit comparators, independent reference judgments where needed, and a predeclared analysis and stopping policy. The contribution is a method for determining exactly what a result supports, including negative findings and unresolved claims. No number of structural checks is treated as proof of unrestricted semantic correctness or practical superiority.

**Keywords:** evidence; inference; falsification; claim scope; evaluation; counterexample.

## 1. Research question and evidential distinctions

Knowledge systems produce many objects that resemble evidence: citations, validation reports, explanations, benchmark scores, approvals, and execution logs. Their resemblance does not make them interchangeable. A citation can establish provenance without establishing truth. Approval can establish permission without establishing correctness. A passing test can establish one observed behavior without establishing all possible behaviors.

We ask: **how can a scientific and engineering claim remain accountable to the kind, scope, and limitations of its actual grounds?** Our answer is a claim-centered method connecting each assertion to its premises, evaluators, counterexamples, and revision conditions.

Program-logic reasoning already makes correctness conditional on stated preconditions and semantics [1]. PROV-DM provides a vocabulary for the origins and production of entities [2]. We use those distinctions while addressing a further operational problem: evidence about a source, model, checker, implementation, and deployment must not silently migrate between those subjects.

## 2. Claims and independent assessment axes

A **claim** is a proposition or quantified assertion with an identified subject, scope, and interpretation. A useful record specifies its content, revision, claim kind, assumptions, supporting grounds, potential refuters, assessment method, and unresolved dependencies. The scope includes relevant versions, task distribution, population, time interval, and operating conditions when those matter.

Claim kinds include definitional, deductive, empirical, comparative, causal, and normative. These are not mutually exclusive labels for an entire document: a paper can define a model, prove a property of it, and propose an empirical hypothesis. Each assertion needs the appropriate grounds.

We distinguish at least five questions:

- Is the source or record authentic and correctly attributed?
- Is its represented content faithful to that source?
- Does the evidence support the proposition under the stated interpretation?
- Is the proposition applicable to the current use?
- Is the proposed use authorized?

These assessments need not agree. An accurately attributed false statement can be useful as an object of refutation. A true proposition can be inapplicable to a different date. An authorized decision can be poorly supported. A single confidence score cannot preserve these distinctions unless its meaning and aggregation rule explicitly encode them.

Unknown is not false, and absence of a recorded refutation is not confirmation. An assessment can remain unresolved while a restricted informational output is permitted. A mandatory action precondition, however, is not satisfied merely because the system failed to find contrary evidence.

## 3. Justification graphs and conditional soundness

A **justification graph** contains claim nodes, premise nodes, and explicit inference applications. An inference application identifies its rule, input claims, output claim, and relevant side conditions. It may also carry a computation or verification witness. The graph is an explanation of the offered justification, not a complete record of every internal mental or computational event.

For the following result, take a finite directed acyclic derivation graph. Each leaf is a premise, and every other claim is obtained by a supported inference rule whose inputs appear earlier in a topological order. Interpret truth in one fixed domain model.

### Proposition 1. Sound rules preserve true premises in a finite derivation

If every premise is true in the model, every used rule is sound for that model when its side conditions hold, and every side condition of every inference application is satisfied in the model, every derived claim is true in that model.

**Proof.** Order the graph topologically. The initial premise nodes are true by assumption. At each subsequent node, all input claims are true by the induction hypothesis and all side conditions hold by assumption. Soundness of that rule under those conditions makes the output true. Induction covers every node. ∎

The result is conditional on premises, rule semantics, and discharged side conditions. For example, a rule that cancels a divisor cannot be used when its nonzero-divisor condition is false. The proposition does not show that the premises were correctly extracted, that the domain model is adequate, or that the implementation actually followed the rule. A computation with correctly applied arithmetic and an incorrectly read input remains an incorrect domain result.

Circular support is outside this proof. A cycle that repeats a conclusion as its own premise supplies no independent foundation. Some systems admit explicitly defined fixed-point reasoning, but they require a separate semantics and correctness argument. Finite serialization of a cycle is not that argument.

### Proposition 2. A countermodel refutes a universal claim within its scope

For a claim \(\forall x\in D, P(x)\), an admissible \(x_0\in D\) with \(\neg P(x_0)\) refutes the claim.

**Proof.** Universal quantification requires \(P\) for every element of \(D\), including \(x_0\). ∎

The domain condition matters. A failure outside the claimed domain can motivate a broader study but does not refute the narrower theorem. Conversely, narrowing the domain after seeing a failure produces a revised claim; it does not retroactively turn the original claim into a successful one.

## 4. What different checks establish

Structural validation establishes compliance with a declared representation rule when the validator is correct. It may verify mandatory fields, references, enumerations, or finite relational conditions. It does not establish that a field's natural-language content is correctly interpreted.

A formal proof establishes a consequence under its assumptions and formal semantics. Executable tests establish observed behavior for the tested cases, subject to their oracle and execution conditions. An exhaustive finite test can support a universal statement about its enumerated finite model; it does not automatically cover unbounded inputs or a deployed environment with different behavior.

Human review can assess interpretation, relevance, and argument quality within a defined rubric. Its strength depends on expertise, access to sources, independence, and adjudication. Production observations can reveal actual failures and costs, but selected observations alone do not identify a causal benefit.

These evidence types are complementary, not a single ladder on which more items always mean stronger support. Ten tests sharing the same incorrect oracle can repeat one mistake. Two reviewers with access to each other's answer may not provide the intended independent judgment. Independence should be described as a concrete procedure, not inferred from different agent names.

## 5. A falsification protocol

An evaluation begins by freezing the claim and requirements before examining the decisive cases. The record should specify the subject versions, admissible inputs, expected behavior, failure criteria, scope of generalization, and how uncertain cases will be handled. Changes create a new evaluation record rather than overwriting the earlier one.

Counterexamples should target assumptions. For extraction, change a referent, logical scope, or source revision. For composition, omit a jointly required ground or add a conflicting condition. For reuse, change a material dependency or current permission. For resource accounting, repeat a telemetry event or expose a shared cost counted twice. The purpose is to distinguish a system that enforces the condition from one that merely passes familiar positive examples.

An independent impact oracle is especially important for dependency completeness. Traversing an annotated graph can verify traversal; it cannot reveal an omitted dependency if the reference answer is generated from that same graph. A useful test mutates a relevant source feature and independently specifies whether the output should change.

The protocol should separate at least four failure classes: the model's claim is false; the implementation violates the model; the input interpretation or annotation is wrong; or the evaluation lacks enough information to decide. Combining them into a single failure total can obscure the repair required.

## 6. Comparators, analysis, and stopping

A comparative claim needs a relevant baseline with matched tasks, resource accounting, and acceptance conditions. A method that produces more complete outputs at a higher cost must not be described as unconditionally cheaper. A method that skips required checks must not gain an apparent speed advantage without reporting the lost assurance.

The analysis plan should state the primary outcome, denominators, treatment of missing and unresolved cases, uncertainty estimates, and stopping conditions. If evaluation stops after a budget limit, the report should identify the covered and uncovered cases. Selecting only successful attempts or changing the success rule after inspection creates a different evidential object from the planned study.

Data separation also matters. Cases used to design a rule, tune a threshold, or repair an implementation are development evidence. A later evaluation can reuse them for regression testing, but should not label that reuse an unseen assessment. Shared sources, repeated near-duplicates, and leaked reference annotations can make nominally separate cases dependent.

For causal or practical-effect claims, the required design is stronger than a catalog of passing controls. The [knowledge utility paper](../knowledge-utility/PAPER.md) states the intervention and attribution conditions. Observed use, user approval, or a plausible demonstration alone does not establish that the system improved outcomes relative to a meaningful alternative.

## 7. The evidential status of this research program

The scientific results presented here are definitions, explicit conditional arguments, and countermodels. They specify what a compliant knowledge process would have to preserve. Published engineering studies provide the separate place for reproducible executions, actual observations, and their limits. A link to a study is not a declaration that every proposed experiment has been performed; the study's own methods and evidence record determine its scope.

This distinction allows a formal or methodological paper to have a complete argument while practical hypotheses remain open. Completion means that the paper states and supports its actual contribution, not that every imaginable deployment question has been settled. Conversely, removing a provisional label cannot substitute for missing evidence.

A falsifiable engineering claim must identify an implementation \(I\), its revision \(v\), a well-formed input domain \(D\), and a mandatory condition \(C\). The claim to test is

\[
\forall x\in D:\quad \neg C(x)
\Rightarrow \operatorname{Admit}(I_v,x)\ne\mathsf{success}.
\]

Here \(D\) includes validly formed negative cases; it is not prefiltered to inputs already satisfying \(C\). A single input in \(D\) that violates \(C\) and nevertheless receives successful admission refutes the claim about that implementation revision. No premise assumes that the implementation already enforces the condition. Positive controls separately test whether permitted cases can succeed, because refusal of every case would satisfy this exclusion property without showing usefulness. Semantic and practical predictions require independently interpreted or measured outcomes in addition to structural controls.

The [contract profile conformance study](../../engineering/studies/contract-profile-conformance/STUDY.md), [source transcription and uncertainty study](../../engineering/studies/source-transcription-and-uncertainty/STUDY.md), and [reuse admission and cost controls study](../../engineering/studies/reuse-admission-and-cost-controls/STUDY.md) address distinct subjects. The [editorial skill pilot](../../engineering/studies/editorial-skill-pilot/STUDY.md) addresses document production. Their results should remain separate until a justified analysis explains how they combine.

## 8. Scope and shared definitions

The model does not automate the discovery of every relevant premise, guarantee the trustworthiness of a reviewer, or establish universal usefulness of structured knowledge. It makes these dependencies inspectable and supplies conditions under which a particular claim can be rejected, retained, or narrowed.

The shared [glossary](../../reference/glossary/GLOSSARY.md) defines evidence, verification, authority, and related terms. [Revision and governance](../revision-and-governance/PAPER.md) specifies how new evidence changes assessments without erasing their history.

## References

1. Hoare, C. A. R. “An Axiomatic Basis for Computer Programming.” *Communications of the ACM* 12(10), 1969, 576–580, 583. <https://doi.org/10.1145/363235.363259>
2. W3C. *PROV-DM: The PROV Data Model*. W3C Recommendation, 2013. <https://www.w3.org/TR/prov-dm/>

artifact: "scientific-paper"

# Interaction, Documents, and Memory with Explicit Revision Boundaries

**Author:** Pavel Valentov · **Publication edition:** 1.1

## Abstract

An assistant's conversation, work state, documents, and durable memory evolve at different rates and under different rules. Treating them as one expanding transcript makes corrections, delayed results, and changes of purpose difficult to interpret. This paper defines a state model that separates messages from communicative acts, Work Items from execution attempts, and current task context from admitted memory. Documents are versioned outputs whose correctness must be checked after rendering or revision. We establish conditional properties for generation boundaries, idempotent event handling, and narrow update scope, and apply the corpus's general dependency-directed reassessment result. Counterexamples identify where correct state handling still permits semantic error. The model also distinguishes observed task success from learning and user acceptance from evidence. Its contribution is a coherent set of transition obligations for dialogue and memory, with falsifiable implementation consequences. It does not establish that any current interpreter reliably recognizes every intention, referent, or learning outcome.

**Keywords:** dialogue; Work Item; document revision; memory; requirements; learning assessment.

## 1. Research question

A conversation can contain instructions, quotations, corrections, tentative ideas, and reports of previous actions. It can also receive a delayed result after the user has changed the goal. If every new message simply overwrites one mutable state, the system cannot reliably explain which requirements a result satisfies or why a memory applies.

We ask: **how should interaction state change so that the scope of a statement, correction, or result remains inspectable?** The proposed answer separates interpretation from authorized transition, and separates immediate dialogue context from persistent knowledge.

The model builds on ordinary distinctions between activities, entities, and agents represented in PROV-DM [1]. It adds explicit obligations for dialogue generations, document outputs, and admission into memory. These obligations are a design model. Their practical adequacy must be assessed against independently interpreted conversations, not inferred from the existence of a structured event log.

## 2. Messages, acts, and work

A **message** is a received or generated representation with sender attribution, time, and content. A **communicative act** is an interpreted function of some content: a request, correction, question, proposal, acknowledgment, or report. One message can contain several acts. An act record should identify its target, scope, and uncertainty.

Quoted content is not automatically an instruction from the present speaker. Likewise, a statement that an action occurred is evidence of that report, not proof of execution. Interpretation assigns a candidate role; authorization determines whether the resulting transition is permitted.

A **Work Item** is a bounded objective with requirements, constraints, expected outputs, and acceptance conditions. `WorkItem` is a serialization spelling of that same entity, not a second concept. An **attempt** is one execution toward the objective. A document or other **output** is a result of an attempt. A message can create or modify a Work Item without itself constituting an execution attempt.

Let the active work state be

\[
W=(i,g,R,C,O,A),
\]

where \(i\) is identity, \(g\) is a requirement generation, \(R\) requirements, \(C\) constraints, \(O\) output expectations, and \(A\) acceptance conditions. A material change creates a new generation whose identifier is never reissued within that Work Item, including after rollback or restoration of earlier requirements. A strictly increasing counter is one implementation. A requirements-content hash alone does not distinguish a later return to earlier content. This discipline does not require abandoning all previous work: an earlier output can be re-evaluated for the new generation, with its original provenance preserved.

Understanding, agreement, evidence, and authority remain separate. A user can understand an answer and reject its conclusion. An authorized owner can accept a document despite an acknowledged evidential limitation. Neither act changes the truth of its factual propositions by definition.

## 3. Context and memory layers

We distinguish immediate conversation context, the current Work Item state, durable user or project memory, and externally sourced knowledge. The distinction is operational: each layer has different admission, revision, scope, and retrieval rules. Storing everything in one database does not eliminate these differences.

A memory record contains content, provenance, applicability scope, assessment state, and a revision or revocation relation. A statement intended for one task does not become a permanent preference merely because it is repeated. Widening its scope requires a justified transition, such as an explicit instruction or a separately supported generalization.

Memory admission is therefore a decision with conditions. Relevant conditions can include attribution, intended persistence, consistency with current instructions, permission to retain, and a defined use scope. Unknown mandatory conditions do not count as satisfied. Retrieval later must check whether the record remains applicable to the present task and recipient.

Memory has dependencies. A derived preference, summary, or plan may rely on earlier records whose revision changes its basis. Revocation does not always imply physical deletion: a historical record may remain necessary for an audit while being inadmissible for current guidance. Current use, archival availability, and storage retention must be modeled separately.

## 4. Documents as evaluated outputs

A document is a versioned representation, not merely a string emitted during conversation. Its assessment has several axes: source fidelity, factual support, satisfaction of requirements, internal consistency, readability, accessibility, and acceptance for a specific use. A fluent document can fail a mandatory requirement; a faithful transcription can be poorly organized.

Generation and editing therefore have a final-output obligation. The actual document must be inspected after assembly, including headings, references, tables, images, and any constraints that can be lost during rendering. Checking only the retrieved sources or an intermediate outline leaves the delivered output unevaluated.

A local edit also has both local and global effects. Replacing one section can change cross-references, definitions, totals, or the force of a conclusion elsewhere. The edit record should state its intended scope, while dependency information identifies the additional checks needed. A claim that “only one paragraph changed” is not evidence that every unaffected-looking paragraph remains valid.

Document summaries become a separate memory object when reused. A summary may omit details acceptable for its original audience but necessary for a later task. Reuse should apply the same applicability and dependency discipline as any other prepared knowledge.

## 5. Conditional transition properties

### Proposition 1. Generation admission prevents a specified stale update

Suppose a result carries the Work Item identity and generation for which it was produced, and generation identifiers are unique and never reissued within that Work Item. Suppose the acceptance transition checks those identifiers against the active state at the same serialization point at which it makes the result current. Direct acceptance requires an exact match; otherwise a separate successful re-evaluation bound to the active generation is required. Then a result from an earlier generation cannot be accepted as current solely through its original completion event.

**Proof.** Non-reuse of generation identifiers makes an earlier generation different from the active one, even if the requirements later return to earlier content. Its completion event therefore fails the direct-admission comparison at acceptance. The only remaining path requires a new evaluation, so original completion alone is insufficient. ∎

This property depends on correct identifiers and enforcement at the transition boundary. It does not guarantee that the interpreter recognized a material requirement change or that the re-evaluation is semantically correct.

### Proposition 2. Recorded duplicate events need not duplicate effects

Let an event handler serialize the check and creation of a stable event identifier so that at most one first commit for that identifier can succeed. The identifier, payload fingerprint, result or stable result reference, and internal effect commit in one atomic state change. A concurrent contender that loses the first-commit race performs no committed effect and reads the winning record. Subsequent handling of the same identifier and payload returns the stored result without applying the effect again; a different payload is rejected as a conflict.

**Proof.** Serialization permits at most one successful first commit. Atomicity binds that commit's effect to its identifier, fingerprint, and result, and prevents an aborted contender from retaining an internal effect. Every later successful lookup takes the duplicate or conflict branch, neither of which applies a new effect. The duplicate branch can return the result because that result or its stable reference was committed with the identifier. ∎

A unique identifier constraint can enforce the first-commit rule only when a conflicting transaction rolls back its whole internal effect and then reads the committed record. Independent atomic transactions without that uniqueness or serialization condition are insufficient: two first deliveries can both observe absence. The result establishes idempotent handling within the stated state boundary, not exactly-once execution of an external action whose system lies outside it. Fingerprint equality also presupposes the declared canonical payload comparison and collision assumptions.

### Dependency-directed reassessment

The general reachability result is stated once in [Revision and governance, Proposition 3](../revision-and-governance/PAPER.md#proposition-3-complete-dependencies-identify-candidates-for-reassessment). Applied to memory and documents, it identifies output assessments that may need reconsideration after a represented dependency changes. The premise is a complete finite dependency graph; the conclusion is candidate identification, not a verdict that the affected output is now false. An omitted dependency defeats that premise even if traversal is correct.

### Proposition 3. A narrow update preserves independent state components

Let a transition write only a declared component set \(J\) of a product state and leave all other components unchanged. Then every observation depending solely on components outside \(J\) is preserved.

**Proof.** Such an observation receives identical component values before and after the transition. ∎

This syntactic preservation result requires correct scope. If two components are semantically coupled but that coupling is omitted, unchanged bytes do not guarantee an unchanged valid interpretation.

## 6. Learning, correction, and counterexamples

A useful tutoring conversation distinguishes the learner's own attempt, supplied assistance, observed performance, and the assessment being made. Repeating an explanation or accepting an answer does not establish learning. A learning claim requires a declared skill, assessment conditions, a time horizon, and evidence that discriminates retained ability from immediate assistance or memorized responses.

This is a measurement obligation rather than a theory of human learning. Different studies may examine transfer, delayed retention, error correction, or independent task completion. Their outcomes should not be collapsed into one unqualified “learned” flag.

Counterexamples test the boundaries of dialogue interpretation. A correction to one branch must not silently rewrite another branch. An ambiguous “use that version” should not become a verified referent without resolution. A delayed answer must not reinstate a withdrawn requirement. A quoted instruction must not acquire the authority of its surrounding message merely because it uses imperative wording.

Further counterexamples concern memory and documents. A task-specific choice should not become an unrestricted preference; a revoked premise should not remain an unnoticed basis for a recommendation; and a successful local edit should not waive whole-document consistency checks. These cases can be specified without assuming any particular domain or model.

## 7. Evidence and limits

The model supports finite transition tests and independently annotated dialogue cases. A useful evaluation reports interpretation mistakes separately from transition-handling mistakes. Otherwise a correct event processor can hide a poor interpreter, or an accurate interpreter can be blamed for an unrelated state-management defect.

This paper establishes only its conditional state properties. It reports no measured improvement in learning, long-term recall, or agent reliability. Structural validation cannot authenticate a speaker by itself, and a requirements map cannot prove that the final document satisfies its intended meaning. Those dependencies require their own evidence and must remain visible in a claim of end-to-end performance.

The [executable projections and revision study](../../engineering/studies/executable-projections-and-revision/STUDY.md) evaluates reader boundaries, selection of records for addressed revision, and finite partial projections. It does not test Work Item generation admission or concurrent event idempotence; no engineering study in this corpus supplies implementation evidence for Propositions 1–2. The [editorial skill pilot](../../engineering/studies/editorial-skill-pilot/STUDY.md) compares two produced versions of one text under disclosed editorial requirements. The shared [glossary](../../reference/glossary/GLOSSARY.md), [revision and governance](../revision-and-governance/PAPER.md), and [evidence and falsification](../evidence-and-falsification/PAPER.md) supply related definitions and assessment boundaries.

## References

1. W3C. *PROV-DM: The PROV Data Model*. W3C Recommendation, 2013. <https://www.w3.org/TR/prov-dm/>

title: "Knowledge Utility, Resource Accounting, and Lifecycle Decisions"
artifact: "scientific-paper"
edition: "1.1"

language: "en"

description: "Task-relative benefit, complete resource accounting, comparison baselines and limits of causal inference."
