# Knowledge Utility, Resource Accounting, and Lifecycle Decisions

**Author:** Pavel Valentov · **Publication edition:** 1.1

## Abstract

Knowledge is useful relative to a task, recipient, policy, time horizon, and alternative. Truth, evidence quality, popularity, and utility therefore cannot be represented as one interchangeable score. This paper defines conditional utility and separates measured outcomes, predicted benefit, resource costs, and causal attribution. It gives conservation conditions for shared-cost allocation, shows why complementary contributions are generally nonadditive, and derives a restricted break-even condition for prepared knowledge. Lifecycle decisions are framed in terms of future marginal value subject to mandatory constraints, while storage retention remains distinct from residency and current eligibility. The contribution is an accounting and evaluation model that prevents common forms of double counting and unsupported attribution. It does not supply universal weights, prices, retention thresholds, or empirical claims that structured knowledge is always beneficial. Those quantities require explicit measurement under a stated intervention and comparison policy.

**Keywords:** knowledge utility; causal attribution; resource accounting; marginal value; retention; reuse economics.

## 1. Research question

A fact can be true and irrelevant to a task. A false claim can be useful as an object of investigation without becoming true. An expensive artifact can be worth retaining, while a frequently retrieved one can add little to outcomes. These cases show why “knowledge value” is not a permanent intrinsic score.

We ask: **how should a system assess the contribution and cost of knowledge without confusing truth, exposure, attribution, and usefulness?** The model below treats value as conditional and preserves the units and assumptions of each assessment.

The causal distinction is central. An observed association between access to an artifact and a good outcome does not alone identify the artifact's effect. Causal inference requires assumptions connecting observed data to the intervention being assessed [1]. Our contribution is to carry that discipline into knowledge selection, prepared-artifact reuse, and retention accounting.

## 2. Conditional utility and outcome vectors

For knowledge artifact \(k\), write

\[
V(k\mid q,c,\pi,h,b,r),
\]

where \(q\) is the task or task distribution, \(c\) the consumer and context, \(\pi\) the use and evaluation policy, \(h\) the observation horizon, \(b\) the baseline, and \(r\) the pinned artifact and policy revisions. These conditions are part of the claim. Changing them can change the value being estimated.

An outcome can be a vector: task quality, consumer effort, latency, uncertainty reduction, prevented harm, introduced harm, and future usefulness. Each component needs a definition, unit, observation method, and treatment of missing data. Predicted benefit, measured outcome, and unresolved assessment must remain distinct. Unknown is not zero.

A scalar score is legitimate only with an explicit mapping \(U\) from the outcome vector to a declared scalar scale. That mapping includes weights, trade-offs, and any monetary conversions. Different stakeholders can legitimately choose different mappings. Where no justified common scale exists, a partial comparison such as Pareto dominance is more informative than an unexplained total ranking. Subsequent comparisons keep \(U\) and the conditioning arguments fixed unless a policy change is explicitly being assessed.

Costs must be included once. One can define a net utility that already contains resource penalties, or subtract a separate cost measure from a gross benefit measure. Subtracting the same cost again produces an accounting error. A new weight or tariff policy creates a new assessment, rather than silently rewriting the meaning of an earlier score.

Truth, evidential support, source quality, applicability, and utility remain separate axes. Utility does not authorize a false factual claim, and low utility does not refute a proposition. These distinctions permit honest evaluation of investigation, correction, and rarely needed knowledge.

## 3. Resource accounting

A resource ledger records the operations that actually consume resources. Depending on the system, these can include input and output tokens, processor time, elapsed time, human effort, storage over time, network transfer, index updates, checks, retries, and reconstruction after eviction. The measurement unit and scope must be explicit.

Elapsed latency is not generally the sum of parallel operation durations. Logical artifacts are not physical replicas. A node count is not a monetary cost without a model connecting it to resources. Monetary amounts require a tariff revision, currency, date, and allocation policy. Billed, estimated, and predicted costs should not be merged into one unlabeled total.

A retry can incur real additional cost; a duplicated telemetry event does not. Stable event identity with payload-conflict handling separates these cases. Tokens, their priced estimate, and the corresponding invoice line are different views of a cost, not three independent charges.

### Proposition 1. Conservative shared-cost allocation prevents creation of cost

Let \(\mathcal E\) be a finite set of measured operations in one accounting scope and unit. For each operation \(e\), let its nonnegative cost \(c_e\), nonnegative consumer allocations \(a_{e,1},\ldots,a_{e,n_e}\), and nonnegative unallocated residual \(u_e\) satisfy

\[
\sum_{i=1}^{n_e}a_{e,i}+u_e=c_e.
\]

If every underlying operation and each of its allocated and residual parts are included exactly once, then

\[
\sum_{e\in\mathcal E}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n_e}a_{e,i}+u_e\right)
=\sum_{e\in\mathcal E}c_e.
\]

**Proof.** Sum the per-operation equalities over \(\mathcal E\). Nonnegativity also gives \(a_{e,i}\leq c_e\) and \(u_e\leq c_e\) for every component, so an allocation cannot hide an excessive charge behind a negative share. ∎

The result is an accounting identity, not a uniquely fair allocation rule. It requires a closed accounting scope. Omitting shared infrastructure or counting the same event in two ledgers defeats the premises.

## 4. Contribution and causal attribution

Events such as found, selected, delivered, consumed, cited, and accepted describe different stages. A citation does not show that the cited artifact caused the outcome. Acceptance of a Work Item does not establish the separate usefulness of every attached source.

Let \(Y_w(\pi+k)\) denote the outcome for task \(w\) under a policy that offers artifact \(k\), and \(Y_w(\pi)\) the outcome under the same policy without that intervention. With the scalar mapping \(U\) from §2 and a fixed task distribution \(\mathcal D\), the artifact-specific contribution target is

\[
\Delta V(k)=\mathbb E_{w\sim\mathcal D}
\left[U(Y_w(\pi+k))-U(Y_w(\pi))\right].
\]

The policies differ only in access to \(k\) and its predeclared consequences. A comparison against a different baseline policy \(\pi_b\) is also meaningful, but estimates the whole policy contrast. If the new condition changes the model, review budget, interface, or task selection as well, attributing that contrast specifically to \(k\) requires additional identification. The conditioning arguments of §2 remain fixed in the displayed shorthand.

The expression defines a target; it does not identify it from arbitrary logs. Randomized assignment, paired task designs, or observational adjustment each require their own assumptions about assignment, interference, support, measurement, and the relevant population. Unrecorded selection effects can make a frequently used artifact appear beneficial because it is offered on easier tasks.

Observation, association, prediction, and causal estimate must therefore be labeled separately. A model's predicted gain can guide exploration but cannot be reported as an observed effect. The observation horizon also matters: immediate completion, delayed retention, and later reuse are different outcomes.

## 5. Complementarity, duplication, and exposure

Write \(V_{\mathrm{set}}(S)\) for the utility assessment of offering an artifact set \(S\), with all conditions from §2 fixed. This set function is distinct from the single-artifact notation \(V(k\mid q,c,\pi,h,b,r)\); its singleton values refer to the same assessed single-artifact interventions. Set utility need not equal the sum of its members' utility. Consider a rule and its necessary condition. Neither alone is useful for the permitted task, but the pair is. If

\[
V_{\mathrm{set}}(\varnothing)=V_{\mathrm{set}}(\{a\})=V_{\mathrm{set}}(\{b\})=0,
\qquad V_{\mathrm{set}}(\{a,b\})=1,
\]

then each leave-one-out contribution within the pair is one. Summing those contributions yields two, although the total gain is one. Marginal contribution therefore depends on the reference set, and summing several margins can double-count complementary value.

Redundant copies create a different problem. Splitting one informational artifact into multiple identifiers should not automatically create multiple independent contributions. Attribution requires a declared identity and coalition policy. An allocation rule can distribute a defined total; it cannot discover unobserved outcomes or establish a uniquely fair reward by itself.

Use frequency needs exposure and opportunity denominators. Low use can reflect narrow applicability, limited demand, poor retrieval, restricted access, or an immature observation period. If a ranking system suppresses artifacts with few uses, new artifacts may receive no opportunity to demonstrate value. This is a possible feedback mechanism, not proof that every ranking system exhibits it.

Evaluation can address the problem through a declared exploration budget, independent task samples, and separate reporting for new and mature artifacts. Such choices are policy decisions with costs and assumptions, not universal constants.

## 6. Reuse economics and lifecycle decisions

### Proposition 2. A simple reuse policy has a conditional break-even point

Suppose a valid cold execution costs \(G\) per request. Preparing an equally acceptable reusable artifact costs \(B\) once, and each admitted reuse costs \(c_{\mathrm{hit}}\), including its required checks. These costs are nonnegative and measured in the same unit over a fixed horizon \(h\). With \(N\geq1\) requests, no intervening invalidation, and no other costs, reuse is cheaper exactly when

\[
B+Nc_{\mathrm{hit}}<NG,
\quad\text{equivalently}\quad
N(G-c_{\mathrm{hit}})>B.
\]

**Proof.** Compare the total costs and rearrange the inequality. If \(G\leq c_{\mathrm{hit}}\), a positive preparation cost cannot be recovered under these assumptions. ∎

This result is deliberately narrow. In the [companion study's accounting model](../../engineering/studies/reuse-admission-and-cost-controls/STUDY.md), it is the special case with hit fraction \(\rho=1\), no rebuilds or additional invalidation and storage costs (\(M=I=S=0\)), and \(c_{\mathrm{hit}}=c_v+c_r\), where \(c_v\) is per-request validation and \(c_r\) is additional hit-path cost. Both formulas use the same horizon and cost scope. Storage, invalidation, rebuilding, coordination, failed attempts, and changing demand can alter the calculation. The two routes must satisfy the same relevant acceptance conditions; omitting required checks is not an economic improvement under that contract.

Lifecycle choices concern future marginal benefits, future costs, switching costs, and mandatory constraints. Past acquisition cost belongs in the historical ledger; a large historical expenditure does not by itself justify continued expensive residency.

**Retention** is the storage policy governing preservation or deletion of bytes. **Residency** concerns where those retained bytes are available, including whether reconstruction is needed. Search visibility, background preparation, and current-use eligibility are separate lifecycle decisions. Archiving a retained original, removing it from search, and withdrawing permission to use it therefore have different effects. A rarely used artifact may be necessary for reproducibility or a critical deadline. A negative utility estimate does not alone authorize destroying the only retained original.

Residency policies can use measured demand and rehydration cost, with hysteresis to avoid repeated switching. Thresholds must be calibrated to the workload and constraints. Popularity cannot override revocation, known corruption, or a mandatory scope restriction. The [controlled reuse paper](../controlled-knowledge-reuse/PAPER.md) defines admission before economic ranking.

## 7. Evaluation and falsification

A utility study should predeclare the task distribution, baseline, intervention, outcome rubric, horizon, resource scope, and treatment of unknown outcomes. It should record opportunity to use the knowledge, actual exposure, and downstream consumption separately. An ablation must remove the intended contribution without silently changing unrelated parts of the system.

Useful negative controls include repeated telemetry with one real operation, complementary artifacts whose summed margins overstate total gain, identical knowledge under multiple identifiers, and a policy change bundled with artifact access. Each reveals a specific attribution or accounting error.

For lifecycle policies, evaluation should include rare mandatory tasks, bursts of demand, changes of source state, and reconstruction after eviction, while measuring storage retention, residency, and eligibility separately. Results should state whether costs are observed or modeled and whether the policy was tuned on the same workload. A successful finite control does not establish optimal retention or residency for every future demand pattern.

The results of this paper are a conditional evaluation model, conservation and break-even properties, and counterexamples to additive or popularity-based shortcuts. No universal utility score or measured economic superiority is asserted. The [reuse admission and cost controls study](../../engineering/studies/reuse-admission-and-cost-controls/STUDY.md) contains finite admission counterexamples and exact cost arithmetic on synthetic inputs; it reports no measured model savings or production workload. The [editorial skill pilot](../../engineering/studies/editorial-skill-pilot/STUDY.md) supplies a separate exploratory comparison of document outputs, without a cost estimate.

The shared [glossary](../../reference/glossary/GLOSSARY.md) and [evidence and falsification paper](../evidence-and-falsification/PAPER.md) define the corresponding claim and measurement boundaries.

## References

1. Pearl, J. “Causal Inference in Statistics: An Overview.” *Statistics Surveys* 3, 2009, 96–146. <https://doi.org/10.1214/09-SS057>

title: "Retrieval, Coverage, and Context Composition under Resource Limits"
artifact: "scientific-paper"

# Retrieval, Coverage, and Context Composition under Resource Limits

**Author:** Pavel Valentov · **Publication edition:** 1.1

## Abstract

Retrieving relevant fragments and assembling a sufficient context are different problems. A retrieved fragment can be pertinent while omitting a mandatory exception; a package can cover declared requirements without demonstrating that the search was exhaustive. This paper gives a finite model of requirements, alternative supporting groups, mandatory dependencies, representations, and resource limits. We distinguish search assurance, requirement coverage, composition feasibility, and actual output verification. Conditional results characterize exact finite selection, a set-cover special case, and the limited applicability of a classical submodular-greedy guarantee. Counterexamples show why jointly necessary grounds and shared dependencies defeat common additive assumptions. The contribution is a precise contract for what a retrieval-and-composition result establishes, including meaningful stopping outcomes. Domain evaluations and executable selectors are separate engineering objects; the present argument establishes no general empirical advantage for a retrieval method or language model.

**Keywords:** retrieval; context composition; coverage; dependency closure; resource budget; submodularity.

## 1. The question between retrieval and answering

A retrieval system proposes material that may support an answer. The original retrieval-augmented generation formulation combines external retrieval with a generative model [1]. That architecture does not itself certify that every necessary condition reached the generator or survived its answer.

Our question is: **what can be certified about a bounded context assembled from a declared search result?** We separate four judgments: whether the search procedure examined the required scope; whether the available grounds support the requirements; whether the delivered representation fits the resource constraints; and whether the final answer preserves the required content.

These judgments are related but not interchangeable. Returning ten plausible passages does not prove that an eleventh exception does not exist. Fitting a token limit does not prove that the recipient notices a warning. A completed search does not make contradictory sources consistent.

## 2. Query, scope, and coverage

A query record fixes an intent, referents, time interpretation, requirements, permitted source scope, and answer form. Its requirements are obligations to establish or communicate, rather than merely words to match. A request for one supporting example has different obligations from a request for all examples, a count, or a statement that none exists.

Let \(S\) be the declared source snapshot and \(C\) the returned candidate set. Search assurance concerns the procedure that produced \(C\). An exhaustive finite scan under a specified predicate can support completeness within \(S\); a top-ranked sample generally cannot. Changes to access visibility, scope membership, predicates, or ranking rules can change the answer even if previously returned records are unchanged.

Coverage is assessed relative to a pinned requirement set \(R\). For each requirement we distinguish supported, partial, unsupported, conflicted, and unknown. These are reporting categories with explicit criteria, not a universal many-valued logic. “Unsupported in this package” does not mean false, absent from the whole corpus, or impossible to establish.

Coverage should be recorded at three boundaries: the registered source base, the retrieved candidates, and the delivered package. A fourth check concerns the actual answer or action proposal. Keeping these reports distinct locates loss: missing from the base, missed by retrieval, omitted during composition, or ignored during generation.

## 3. Composition with joint grounds and dependencies

Let \(V\) be a finite set of candidate object revisions. For a selected subset \(A\subseteq V\), let \(\operatorname{cl}(A)\) denote its mandatory dependency closure under a fixed, declared graph. A requirement \(r\) can have alternative supporting groups \(\mathcal G_r\). A group contains jointly necessary grounds, not merely individually relevant fragments.

Under fixed assessments, define support by

\[
\operatorname{Supports}(r,A)
\iff \exists G\in\mathcal G_r:\ G\subseteq\operatorname{cl}(A)
\ \land\ \operatorname{Valid}(G,r).
\]

The validity predicate includes the relevant interpretation and applicability checks. Listing a group does not prove that its members support the requirement. In empirical use, that judgment requires independently assessed evidence or a sound supported rule.

A composition decision \(z\) includes selected content, permitted transformations, ordering, modality, and delivery form. Its actual representation is \(\operatorname{Render}(z)\), with declared cost \(c\). Feasibility requires mandatory support, all other admission conditions, and

\[
c(\operatorname{Render}(z))\leq B.
\]

The budget may be a vector: tokens, bytes, recipient time, or another measured resource. Different units should not be silently added. A scalar objective requires a declared conversion or priority rule.

Costs need not be additive over fragments. Two selected objects can share a dependency, so summing their separately closed sizes double-counts it. Conversely, a delivery format can introduce headings, repeated legends, or fixed overhead. The delivered representation, rather than the unformatted fragments, is the relevant accounting object.

Least graph closure is also relative to the chosen granularity. The [projection study](../../engineering/studies/executable-projections-and-revision/STUDY.md#counterexample-to-claim-level-minimality) found extra claims inside a shared qualification even when their dedicated nodes were absent from the selected closure. A least entity set therefore need not be claim-minimal or cheapest to deliver.

## 4. Exactness and special-case guarantees

### Proposition 1. Complete finite selection gives a scoped optimum

Let \(Z\) be a finite, explicitly declared decision space. Suppose feasibility and objective value are evaluated correctly for every member. If enumeration finishes and returns a feasible member with maximal objective, that member is optimal in \(Z\). If enumeration finishes with no feasible member, no feasible member exists in \(Z\).

**Proof.** Every possible decision in the declared space has been checked. A better feasible decision or any feasible decision in the second case would therefore have appeared in the enumeration, contradicting the stated result. ∎

This establishes neither completeness of \(Z\) relative to all possible summaries nor correctness of semantic annotations. If execution stops early, the best verified candidate remains a feasible incumbent; optimality and infeasibility are no longer established.

### Proposition 2. A restricted composition problem contains set cover

Consider unit-cost, independent packages, no transformations or dependencies, and the requirement to cover every obligation using at most \(k\) packages. Given a set-cover instance with universe \(U\) and subsets \(F_1,\ldots,F_n\), create one requirement per element of \(U\) and one package per \(F_i\), supporting exactly those requirements. The construction is polynomial, and selected packages cover all requirements exactly when their corresponding subsets cover \(U\).

This reduction transfers the known combinatorial difficulty of set cover [2] to this special case. It does not imply that every practical query is difficult, that extraction itself is formally solved, or that a heuristic has a particular approximation ratio.

### Proposition 3. The cardinality-greedy bound requires submodularity

Let \(f\) be a nonnegative, monotone, submodular set function with \(f(\varnothing)=0\). With at most \(k\geq1\) selected elements and no other constraints, greedy selection by greatest marginal gain achieves at least

\[
\left(1-\left(1-\frac1k\right)^k\right)\operatorname{OPT}
\geq (1-1/e)\operatorname{OPT}.
\]

**Argument.** Let \(O\) be an optimal set. At step \(i\), monotonicity gives \(\operatorname{OPT}=f(O)\leq f(A_i\cup O)\). Submodularity bounds the gain from \(A_i\) to \(A_i\cup O\) by the sum of at most \(k\) current single-element marginal gains. The best next gain is therefore at least \((\operatorname{OPT}-f(A_i))/k\). Each step reduces the remaining gap by a factor no greater than \(1-1/k\). Iterating from the empty set gives the bound. This is the classical result of Nemhauser, Wolsey, and Fisher [3], not a new theorem of the present model. ∎

The result does not cover arbitrary size-based greedy selection, nonadditive costs, or arbitrary mandatory closure. Those settings need their own justified algorithms and assumptions.

## 5. Counterexamples to convenient assumptions

Joint grounds need not be submodular. Suppose a requirement needs both a rule \(a\) and a condition \(b\). Let \(f(\varnothing)=f(\{a\})=f(\{b\})=0\), but \(f(\{a,b\})=1\). Adding \(b\) to the empty set yields no gain; adding it after \(a\) yields one. This violates diminishing returns. Individual ranking can therefore discard both members of the only useful pair.

Feasible root selections need not be downward-closed because coverage is mandatory. Consider one requirement whose only supporting group is \(\{a\}\), no dependencies, and a budget large enough for \(a\). The selection \(A=\{a\}\) is feasible, but its subset \(\varnothing\) does not cover the requirement. This counterexample uses the same closure-based support definition as §3.

Selected roots and delivered objects must not be confused. Removing a required condition from a root set can leave its closure unchanged because another selected root still requires it. Removing that condition from the actual delivered set is different: the resulting delivery may fail the closure obligation. A method justified for downward-closed feasible selections cannot be imported without checking which of these objects its guarantee concerns.

Budget pressure does not remove these obligations. If two mandatory grounds and an exception cost seven units in total and the budget is five, deleting the exception produces a smaller but inadmissible package. A feasible reformulation might exist, but it must be constructed and checked. Failure of one heuristic to find it is not proof of impossibility.

Finally, declared coverage can be invalidated by new information. Support is monotone only under explicitly fixed grounds, applicability judgments, and conflict rules. An added contradictory source or a changed requirement can require reassessment even when old passages remain available.

## 6. Stopping and measurement

A useful result reports its scope and stopping reason. Distinct outcomes include a verified complete package, an explicitly partial package, a need for clarification, insufficient resources, an exhausted search limit, and stale or conflicting evidence. Stopping after a limit does not establish absence. A useful partial answer is possible when the answer form permits it and its uncovered obligations remain visible.

Evaluation should compare methods under the same requirement snapshot, source scope, delivery constraints, and outcome rubric. Relevant baselines include ranked selection with dependency repair, selection of complete supporting groups, and exact enumeration on small instances. Removing dependencies or truncating delivery supplies ablations, not necessarily competitive baselines.

Measures should distinguish retrieval misses, invalid support groups, omitted dependencies, budget violations, and final-answer failures. Report denominators and uncertainty, including the number of cases that could not be adjudicated. Counting selected fragments or citing a larger source list does not measure sufficiency.

The mathematical results here hold for the declared finite model. They establish neither universal end-to-end answer correctness nor measured superiority on people, models, or production workloads. Recipient understanding and attention require separate observation, even when representation cost is measured exactly.

## 7. Connections to the published engineering work

The [dependency closure and package delivery study](../../engineering/studies/dependency-closure-and-package-delivery/STUDY.md) supplies exhaustive fixed-graph closure checks for all-required dependencies, plus historical layout and preliminary delivery checks. Its root-subset enumeration is not a budgeted composition optimizer. Alternative supporting groups, resource-constrained selection, and the counterexamples in §5 have no executable selection study in this corpus. The [projection study](../../engineering/studies/executable-projections-and-revision/STUDY.md) supplies the distinct counterexample to claim-level minimality. The [contract profile conformance study](../../engineering/studies/contract-profile-conformance/STUDY.md) concerns structural validation, which must remain distinct from semantic sufficiency.

The [glossary](../../reference/glossary/GLOSSARY.md) gives common terminology. [Evidence and falsification](../evidence-and-falsification/PAPER.md) specifies evaluation obligations; [knowledge utility](../knowledge-utility/PAPER.md) explains why a composition objective must state its baseline, horizon, and resource accounting.

## References

1. Lewis, P., et al. “Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Knowledge-Intensive NLP Tasks.” *Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems* 33, 2020. <https://arxiv.org/abs/2005.11401>
2. Karp, R. M. “Reducibility Among Combinatorial Problems.” In *Complexity of Computer Computations*, 1972, 85–103. <https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4684-2001-2_9>
3. Nemhauser, G. L., Wolsey, L. A., and Fisher, M. L. “An Analysis of Approximations for Maximizing Submodular Set Functions—I.” *Mathematical Programming* 14, 1978, 265–294. <https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01588971>

# Knowledge Revision, Version Identity, and the Adoption of Norms

**Author:** Pavel Valentov · **Publication edition:** 1.1

## Abstract

Knowledge can change because its content is corrected, its evidence is reassessed, its use conditions change, or a community adopts a different rule. These changes should not be represented by one undifferentiated status. This paper separates object identity, content revision, representation, assessment, and normative adoption. It defines revision and admission operations, establishes conditional properties of pinned references and dependency-based reassessment, and explains why the adoption of a norm does not generally establish the truth of the claims it contains. A central distinction is between reproducing a historical state and authorizing a present use. The contribution is an explicit account of what changes, what remains attributable, and which previous checks can legitimately transfer. The account is suitable for finite implementation and counterexample testing, but does not establish complete dependency discovery, authenticated authority, or a universally correct governance policy.

**Keywords:** knowledge revision; provenance; versioning; governance; normative adoption; historical reproducibility.

## 1. Research question

A knowledge repository often uses “current,” “approved,” or “valid” as if each described one property. In practice, a record can be the latest revision while being disputed, an approved policy can contain a factual error, and a historical version can remain necessary for reproducing an old result.

Our question is: **how can a system revise knowledge and adopt norms without confusing content, evidence, and authority?** We propose distinct objects and transition rules for each. The purpose is not to freeze knowledge, but to preserve a clear explanation of why a particular revision or assessment was used at a particular time.

PROV-DM offers the general distinction between entities, activities, and responsible agents [1]. We use that structure to connect revisions and decisions. The additional contribution is a set of obligations for transferring checks, following current versions, retaining historical grounds, and admitting normative decisions.

Dependency-directed revision has an established basis in Doyle's truth-maintenance work, which records reasons for beliefs and revises them when assumptions change [2]. The reachability result below isolates an elementary prerequisite for identifying affected assessments; it does not implement a truth-maintenance system. Alchourrón, Gärdenfors, and Makinson study contraction and revision of logical theories under explicit postulates [3]. Our use of “revision” concerns versioned records, assessments, and authority decisions, and claims neither an AGM revision operator nor satisfaction of those postulates.

## 2. Five different objects of change

An **identity** groups revisions treated as versions of one object under a declared identity policy. The policy matters: a correction, a translation, a derived summary, and a competing interpretation need not all be revisions of the same object.

A **content revision** is a pinned content state. Material content changes create a new revision with a relation to its predecessor. Immutability here is a property of the repository's version discipline, not a guarantee supplied by writing a revision label.

A **representation** is a delivery or serialization of content: a document rendering, machine format, or other declared form. Two representations may preserve selected content while differing in accessibility or layout. Equivalence requires an appropriate preservation check; a shared title is insufficient.

An **assessment** concerns a proposition about a revision: whether it is supported, applicable, conflicted, current for a purpose, or compliant with a rule. New evidence can change an assessment without changing the assessed text. Overwriting the text to reflect every assessment change would confuse the object with judgments about it.

A **normative adoption** is a decision to use a rule within a named community, process, or jurisdiction. It identifies the decision maker, authority basis, scope, effective period, adopted revision, and review or withdrawal conditions. Adoption makes the rule operative under that policy; it does not make every factual premise of the rule true.

These distinctions permit a corrected revision and a withdrawn assessment to coexist with an earlier historical record. They also prevent unrelated properties—such as popularity, technical availability, or a successful build—from acting as substitutes for adoption or evidential support.

## 3. Revision operations and their grounds

A revision operation records the affected object, motivating evidence or request, proposed change, relevant dependencies, decision, and resulting revisions or assessments. A useful operation can correct a transcription, replace a false premise, narrow an applicability condition, add a competing interpretation, or change a normative decision.

Not every conflict should be resolved by replacing one statement. The conflict may concern different dates, referents, scopes, or interpretations. Preserving those distinctions can dissolve an apparent contradiction without declaring either source universally correct. If the conflict remains unresolved, the system should retain that assessment and avoid presenting one branch as established merely because it is newer.

Checks attach to a revision and an assessment scope. A successful check of an old text is not automatically a successful check of a materially changed text. Transfer requires a reason: the checked property is unaffected by the change, the transformation preserves it, or the check has been repeated. The reason should be inspectable rather than inferred from a similar filename.

Duplicate detection is likewise scoped. Equivalent content can be consolidated while retaining multiple provenance relations. Merging identities without a justified identity policy can destroy distinctions between independent sources, conflicting readings, or separate normative authorities.

## 4. Conditional properties

### Proposition 1. Pinned references preserve historical selection

Suppose a reference names an immutable revision, and retrieval either returns that exact revision or reports failure. Changing the repository's current-revision pointer does not change the content selected by that reference.

**Proof.** Selection depends on the named revision, not on the current pointer. The pointer change therefore cannot alter the selected identifier or its immutable content. ∎

This property requires storage integrity and exact reference resolution. A readable label or hash field alone does not ensure either. It also guarantees selection, not perpetual availability, current applicability, or permission to disclose the historical content.

### Proposition 2. Checks transfer through preserved complete inputs

Let a deterministic check \(f\) depend on a complete input tuple \(d\). If two revisions and their evaluation contexts induce the same tuple, then the check returns the same result for both.

**Proof.** Determinism gives equal outputs for equal complete inputs. ∎

The word “complete” is decisive. A check that depends on a policy version, current authorization, or a source interpretation cannot be transferred using only equality of visible text. If the evaluator itself changes, the equality-of-function assumption must also be reconsidered.

### Proposition 3. Complete dependencies identify candidates for reassessment

If every dependency relevant to an assessment is represented in a finite graph, complete reverse reachability from a changed dependency identifies all assessments potentially affected by that change.

**Proof.** A relevant dependency gives a finite path from the assessment to the changed object. Reversal makes the assessment reachable from the change. Complete traversal includes every such path. ∎

This result identifies candidates for reassessment, not automatic failures. An assessment may remain valid because an alternative ground still suffices. Conversely, a missing edge can hide an affected assessment despite a correct traversal algorithm.

### Proposition 4. Adoption is not a truth-preserving inference rule

Let \(A\) be an adoption record and \(F\) a factual proposition in the adopted rule. Suppose \(A\land F\) and \(A\land\neg F\) are both satisfiable under the admitted interpretation. Then the adoption record alone does not entail \(F\).

**Argument.** The satisfying interpretation of \(A\land\neg F\) is a countermodel to \(A\Rightarrow F\). The other interpretation shows that the same adoption record is also compatible with \(F\). Thus adoption alone leaves this contingent factual content undecided. ∎

The independence premise excludes logical validities and facts already entailed by the adoption record, such as a correctly represented fact about the adoption event itself. An additional reliable procedure could supply evidence for \(F\), but its reliability would be a separate premise. This does not deny the practical force of adopted rules; it locates that force in the declared governance policy.

## 5. Authority and normative admission

A governance policy defines who may propose, review, adopt, amend, or withdraw a norm, under which conditions. Those are different permissions. A role description can explain responsibility without granting every operation associated with the role.

Admission should evaluate current authority, the applicable procedure, the exact proposed revision, required reviews, unresolved conflicts, and any effective-date conditions. When a condition is mandatory, unknown is not pass. A record that says a reviewer approved something must be connected to the correct subject and a trustworthy attribution mechanism; a field named “reviewer” cannot authenticate itself.

The model does not prescribe one decision rule for every community. A maintainer decision, a quorum, or an external mandate can each be represented, provided the rule and its scope are explicit. The research obligation is to state what the procedure establishes and what it leaves unproven.

An appeal or correction creates a new event rather than erasing the earlier decision. This permits analysis of why an earlier output was produced and how the current basis differs. Accountability requires that history remain inspectable under the applicable retention and access rules; it does not require unrestricted public disclosure of every underlying record.

## 6. Historical reproduction and current use

Historical reproduction asks what followed from a pinned state and policy. Current use asks whether the result is admissible now. These can have different answers. A historical computation may remain reproducible even when its source has been superseded. A current recommendation may require reassessment despite exact reproduction of the earlier computation.

Time therefore has several meanings: when an event occurred, when it was recorded, when a rule became effective, and when a use is being admitted. Collapsing them into one timestamp makes delayed information and retrospective correction difficult to represent correctly.

Likewise, storage retention, publication status, and use eligibility are distinct. An artifact can remain archived while current use is withdrawn. Removing it from search results does not erase all copies. Retaining an original for reproducibility does not grant permission to use it in a new context. Each transition should identify its intended effect.

Migration between representations introduces another preservation question. A migration report should state what is mapped, what is transformed with checked preservation, what is intentionally omitted, and what cannot be represented. A successful import is not evidence of semantic equivalence if critical distinctions were collapsed.

## 7. Falsification and evidence boundary

The model can be tested with cases that change one material feature: a source revision, authority grant, effective date, interpretation rule, or mandatory dependency. It should also include unchanged content with a changed assessment, and changed layout with preserved content but impaired delivery.

Counterexamples are particularly useful where identifiers conceal assumptions. Reusing a prior receipt after a policy change tests whether the receipt is wrongly treated as permanent permission. Updating a current pointer while resolving a pinned reference tests historical selection. Merging two textually equal records with different provenance tests whether deduplication destroys relevant history.

These controls can establish behavior on finite represented cases. They do not prove complete dependency discovery, correct interpretation of all governance rules, or trustworthy authentication in an unexamined deployment. The formal propositions remain conditional on those boundaries.

The contribution is an explicit account of the stated distinctions between content, assessment, historical selection, and normative adoption, together with checkable consequences and counterexamples. It does not claim that this list exhausts every governance model. Nor does it make an empirical claim that one governance procedure is universally superior or that the model resolves substantive disagreement automatically.

## 8. Connections to the published engineering work

The [executable projections and revision study](../../engineering/studies/executable-projections-and-revision/STUDY.md) concerns pinned revisions and bounded update behavior. The [reuse admission and cost controls study](../../engineering/studies/reuse-admission-and-cost-controls/STUDY.md) examines current eligibility of previously prepared artifacts.

The shared [glossary](../../reference/glossary/GLOSSARY.md), [interaction and memory](../interaction-and-memory/PAPER.md), and [controlled knowledge reuse](../controlled-knowledge-reuse/PAPER.md) define the connected work-state and admission obligations.

## References

1. W3C. *PROV-DM: The PROV Data Model*. W3C Recommendation, 2013. <https://www.w3.org/TR/prov-dm/>
2. Doyle, J. “A Truth Maintenance System.” *Artificial Intelligence* 12(3), 1979, 231–272. [Publisher record](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/0004370279900080). [MIT report version, AI Memo 521](https://dspace.mit.edu/entities/publication/5377b306-4ecc-4687-b1f5-78cbb4a0543a).
3. Alchourrón, C. E., Gärdenfors, P., and Makinson, D. “On the Logic of Theory Change: Partial Meet Contraction and Revision Functions.” *The Journal of Symbolic Logic* 50(2), 1985, 510–530. <https://doi.org/10.2307/2274239>

BLOB papers/semantic-objects-and-context/PAPER.md c3a3473e57ea472aef01613781e412f14d11321e4f05d05e6adf9f4535f697f8 original 17398
---
name: "semantic-objects-and-context"
title: "Semantic Objects, Context, and Conditions of Applicability"
artifact: "scientific-paper"

author: "Pavel Valentov"
language: "en"

description: "A formal account of source interpretations, context, mandatory dependencies and conditions of applicability."

# Semantic Objects, Context, and Conditions of Applicability

**Author:** Pavel Valentov · **Publication edition:** 1.1

## Abstract

A knowledge system must distinguish what a source contains, how that content is interpreted, and whether an interpretation supports a particular use. This paper specifies a model that keeps those questions separate. Its basic unit is a versioned semantic object with an attributable interpretation, explicit context, and inspectable grounds. Applicability is a decision relative to declared requirements, rather than a permanent property of a passage. We establish five elementary conditional results: source fidelity does not establish truth; a finite dependency graph has a least required closure; mandatory strengthening narrows certified applicability; additional possible readings narrow common consequences; and complete backward slicing preserves selected deterministic outputs. Counterexamples show why omitted dependencies, ambiguous scope, and unexamined modal distinctions defeat stronger conclusions. These are properties of the stated model, not a proof that an interpreter extracts all relevant meaning. The contribution is an integrated, falsifiable account of the obligations between extraction and use. Reproducible transcription and dependency studies are published separately from the scientific argument.

**Keywords:** semantic object; provenance; context; applicability; interpretation; dependency closure.

## 1. Research question and contribution

An accurate quotation can be false. A true statement can be irrelevant to a task. Two identical sentences can concern different objects, dates, or obligations. A system that stores only text and a confidence score cannot express these differences without assigning several incompatible meanings to the same field.

Our question is: **what must remain explicit when source material becomes knowledge offered for a particular task?** We propose a separation between source representation, interpretation, grounding, applicability, and delivery. The contribution is the organization of these obligations and the identification of their failure conditions. The mathematical results below are elementary consequences of the definitions; we do not claim a new general theory of meaning.

The model complements established work. W3C PROV-DM describes provenance through entities, activities, and agents [1]. SKOS provides concepts, labels, and relations for knowledge organization [2]. Neither a provenance link nor a concept label is, by itself, evidence that a proposition is true or applicable. We use those distinctions while adding a task-relative admission question: what has to hold before this interpretation may support this use?

## 2. Objects, interpretations, and grounds

A **source representation** is a particular version of material: text, image, table, audio, or a combination. A locator identifies a region within that representation. A locator can make inspection repeatable; it does not certify the correctness of the region's interpretation.

A **semantic object** is a record

\[
o=(i,v,m,A,C,G),
\]

where \(i\) is identity, \(v\) is revision, \(m\) is the represented content, \(A\) is attribution, \(C\) is context, and \(G\) is a set of grounds and dependency relations. Content may represent a proposition, question, instruction, constraint, example, or another declared role. A role is not an epistemic verdict: recording an instruction does not establish its legitimacy, and recording an example does not establish a universal rule.

An **interpretation** maps a source region to such a record under declared reading assumptions. Interpretation can be uncertain or plural. If a diagram permits two readings, the record can retain both, with the observations needed to resolve them. Choosing one silently changes the knowledge claim.

**Grounding** identifies why a particular part of the interpretation is supported. A source may support that an author asserted \(P\), while an independent measurement supports \(P\) itself. These are different grounds. We therefore distinguish

\[
\operatorname{Says}(a,P) \quad\text{and}\quad P.
\]

There is no inference from the first to the second without additional premises about the source and the relevant domain. Co-occurrence is likewise not an implication: placing two statements in one paragraph does not establish a causal or logical relation between them.

Extraction must preserve logical structure when it matters. For a domain with two objects, one satisfying \(P\) and one not, “not every object satisfies \(P\)” is true while “every object fails \(P\)” is false. Similarly, absence of an obligation to act is different from an obligation not to act. A semantic representation that merges these distinctions changes the claim even if it preserves most words.

## 3. Context and applicability

We distinguish six context roles: source context, including terminology, referents, and local legends; extraction context, including the inspected representation and unresolved details; request context, including intent, completeness requirements, and budget; recipient context, including usable forms and modalities; situation context, including the target object, time, and reference frame; and intended-action context, specifying what is to be explained, compared, computed, or executed. These are functional roles, not a requirement to duplicate a value in six places. A single linked value can serve several roles, while source conditions and target conditions remain distinguishable.

Let a use request be \(q\), a relevant state be \(s\), and the finite declared mandatory conditions be \(R(q)\). Each condition has an assessment

\[
e_r(o,q,s)\in\{\mathsf{pass},\mathsf{fail},\mathsf{unknown},\mathsf{not\_applicable}\}.
\]

A condition is **discharged** when it passes, or when a justified applicability rule establishes that it imposes no obligation in the exact assessed scope. Write \(w_r(o,q,s)\) for the validity of that rule application and its supporting witness. Then

\[
\operatorname{Discharged}_r(o,q,s)
\iff e_r(o,q,s)=\mathsf{pass}
\ \lor\ \bigl(e_r(o,q,s)=\mathsf{not\_applicable}\land w_r(o,q,s)\bigr).
\]

The witness identifies the rule, its grounds, and the subject and scope to which it applies. A bare `not_applicable` label is insufficient. Certified applicability is

\[
\operatorname{Applicable}(o,q,s)
\iff \forall r\in R(q),\ \operatorname{Discharged}_r(o,q,s).
\]

This definition uses the same four assessment values as the [status model](../../engineering/specifications/status-model/SPECIFICATION.md). It does not treat unknown as pass or allow an unsupported exemption. It also does not equate failure to certify with proof that the content is false. An unanswered question about a relevant date may block a current recommendation while leaving the underlying statement unresolved.

Certification is only as strong as the completeness and correctness of the declared conditions, assessments, and exemption rules. If \(R(q)=\varnothing\), the displayed quantification is vacuously true; it does not establish that the request had no material obligations. Using certification to justify a consequential use therefore requires a separate justification that the requirement set covers that use.

Conditions can be inherited only through a declared rule. A section-level exception may apply to its paragraphs, but arbitrary proximity does not establish inheritance. Conflicting conditions require an explicit conflict decision or a restricted answer. A system must not select whichever condition produces a convenient result.

The request determines the necessary depth. A short identification question may require fewer dependencies than an instruction to execute a consequential action. Increasing depth can create new obligations; it is not merely a demand for a longer response. Applicability must also be checked for the actual recipient and delivered representation. A condition available to the system but absent from the delivered instructions may not protect the recipient.

## 4. Conditional results

### Proposition 1. Fidelity cannot discriminate indistinguishable worlds

Suppose two possible worlds produce the same observed source representation, while \(P\) is true in one and false in the other. Any deterministic procedure using only that representation produces the same decision in both worlds. It therefore cannot correctly decide the truth of \(P\) in both.

**Proof.** The procedure receives identical inputs, so its outputs coincide. The correct truth verdicts differ. Hence at least one verdict is incorrect if the procedure returns a definite truth value. A randomized procedure has the same output distribution in both worlds and likewise receives no discriminating evidence. ∎

This result does not make sources useless. It identifies the extra premise needed to move from faithful attribution to a truth claim: a reliable domain model, independent observation, or another justified discriminating ground.

### Proposition 2. Least required closure exists on a finite graph

Let \(V\) be a finite set of pinned object revisions, \(\mathit{Roots}\subseteq V\) selected roots, and \(D_q\subseteq V\times V\) the active mandatory dependencies for a fixed request. Define

\[
C_0=\mathit{Roots},\qquad
C_{n+1}=C_n\cup\{v:\exists u\in C_n,(u,v)\in D_q\}.
\]

Then the sequence reaches a fixed point \(C^*\), which is the least \(D_q\)-closed set containing \(\mathit{Roots}\).

**Proof.** The sequence is increasing and contained in finite \(V\), so only finitely many strict additions are possible. Its fixed point is closed by construction. For any closed set \(S\) containing \(\mathit{Roots}\), induction gives \(C_n\subseteq S\) for every \(n\). Thus \(C^*\subseteq S\). ∎

Cycles do not invalidate this set-theoretic result. The [tested engineering profile](../../engineering/studies/dependency-closure-and-package-delivery/STUDY.md) rejects cycles; its executable evidence therefore covers the acyclic profile, while the cyclic case here is supported by the proof. Missing edges invalidate an interpretation of computed closure as semantic completeness: a perfectly computed closure can still omit an unrecorded exception.

“Least” is relative to the declared vertices and edges. The [projection study's counterexample](../../engineering/studies/executable-projections-and-revision/STUDY.md#counterexample-to-claim-level-minimality) shows that a least entity closure can still carry unnecessary claims inside a shared qualification. The proposition establishes neither claim-level minimality nor a smallest token budget.

### Proposition 3. Mandatory strengthening narrows certified applicability

Let \(R\subseteq R'\), and keep the assessments, exemption witnesses, and discharge semantics of shared conditions unchanged. Every object certified under \(R'\) is certified under \(R\).

**Proof.** Discharging every condition in \(R'\) entails discharging every condition in its subset \(R\). ∎

The fixed-semantics condition matters. If adding a requirement also changes the meaning of an earlier one, the comparison is no longer a simple strengthening.

### Proposition 4. Additional readings narrow common consequences

For a nonempty set of possible readings \(I\), let \(K(I)=\bigcap_{i\in I}\operatorname{Conseq}(i)\), with a fixed consequence relation. If \(I\subseteq I'\), then \(K(I')\subseteq K(I)\).

**Proof.** Membership in the intersection over \(I'\) requires membership in every set indexed by \(I\), as well as any additional sets. ∎

This supports conservative reporting under ambiguity. It does not establish that the recorded readings include the correct one, or that the consequence relation is sound for the domain.

### Proposition 5. A complete backward slice preserves selected deterministic outputs

Let a finite acyclic computation graph have fixed deterministic node functions and fixed input values. Retain the selected output nodes and every node on which they depend. Evaluating that retained graph gives the same selected outputs as evaluating the full graph.

**Proof.** Order the retained graph topologically. Retained input nodes have unchanged values. Every later retained node has all its predecessors retained and, by induction, receives their unchanged values. Its fixed function therefore returns the same value. This includes the selected outputs. ∎

The result concerns complete computational dependencies. Hidden inputs, changing functions, omitted edges, or an inadequate model of the original task defeat its premises. It is a restricted acyclic instance of the behavior-preservation obligation studied in program slicing [3], not a new slicing algorithm. Applying that obligation to knowledge additionally requires a justified interpretation and task scope; the result does not prove that an arbitrary textual summary preserves meaning.

## 5. Granularity, identity, and delivery

There is no task-independent ideal size for a semantic object. A large unit may hide an exception; a tiny unit may detach a predicate from its scope. A useful decomposition exposes independent revision and inspection while retaining explicit mandatory connections. Granularity is therefore evaluated against reconstruction and use obligations, not by a preferred number of words.

Identity, equivalent content, and interchangeable use must also remain separate. Two records can contain equivalent claims but have different provenance. Two revisions of one record can differ on a critical condition. Two objects interchangeable for one question can be distinguishable for another. Deduplication should consolidate a verified content class without discarding the provenance that supports or qualifies its members.

Delivery creates a further obligation. A preserved image identifier does not guarantee that a recipient receives a readable image; alternative text does not necessarily preserve a visual relation needed by the task. The delivered package must include or expose the relevant material at a usable resolution and with the necessary legend or interpretation. These are inspectable engineering conditions, not consequences of naming a multimodal field.

## 6. Falsification and scope of evidence

The propositions establish properties of declared representations. The harder empirical question is whether an extraction process supplies the right representations and dependencies. That question requires independently prepared cases and adjudication rules. A useful evaluation distinguishes transcription error, attribution error, logical-scope error, omitted context, and unsupported inference. A single aggregate accuracy score hides these different failure mechanisms.

Counterexamples should include a changed referent with unchanged wording, an exception outside the extracted region, an ambiguous visual mark, and a correct quotation of a false assertion. An independent reference annotation should specify what counts as a permissible alternative reading. Agreement between two outputs is insufficient when both inherit the same omitted condition.

This article establishes the stated conditional properties and a falsifiable specification of extraction obligations. It reports no measured superiority of a particular model, annotation process, or user interface. Executable examples can test compliance on finite inputs; they cannot prove complete interpretation of unrestricted language or images.

## 7. Connections to the published engineering work

The [source transcription and uncertainty study](../../engineering/studies/source-transcription-and-uncertainty/STUDY.md) demonstrates that a reproducible projection can omit recorded uncertainty, and reports a correction that preserves that field. It does not evaluate plural interpretations or the correctness of source locators. Those extraction obligations remain untested by the published studies. The [dependency closure and package delivery study](../../engineering/studies/dependency-closure-and-package-delivery/STUDY.md) separates fixed-graph closure from historical delivery checks; it does not establish that the graph contains every semantic dependency.

Definitions shared across the project are indexed in the [glossary](../../reference/glossary/GLOSSARY.md). The next scientific steps are [constrained transformations](../constrained-transformations/PAPER.md) and [retrieval and context composition](../retrieval-and-context-composition/PAPER.md), which use the applicability and dependency obligations defined here.

## References

1. W3C. *PROV-DM: The PROV Data Model*. W3C Recommendation, 2013. <https://www.w3.org/TR/prov-dm/>
2. W3C. *SKOS Simple Knowledge Organization System Reference*. W3C Recommendation, 2009. <https://www.w3.org/TR/skos-reference/>
3. Weiser, M. “Program Slicing.” *IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering* SE-10(4), 1984, 352–357. [Original article](https://faculty.cc.gatech.edu/~harrold/6340/cs6340_fall2009/Readings/Weiser.Slicing.TSE.pdf).

# Primary Sources and Research Foundations

**Publication edition:** 1.1 · **Bibliographic verification:** 4 November 2026

The entries below identify the primary sources actually used by the current edition. They distinguish established results and external formats from this project's own synthesis. A cited source supplies the stated conceptual or technical foundation; it does not endorse Knowledge Contract or establish the effectiveness of its implementation. No third-party book text or source-page images are reproduced in this publication.

## Provenance

Luc Moreau and Paolo Missier, editors. **PROV-DM: The PROV Data Model.** W3C Recommendation, 30 April 2013. [Dated specification](https://www.w3.org/TR/2013/REC-prov-dm-20130430/).

Used for the distinction among entities, activities and agents in provenance, and for the separation of provenance from a judgment about content. The project adds task-specific applicability and admission obligations; those are not claims made by PROV-DM on its behalf.

## SKOS

Alistair Miles and Sean Bechhofer, editors. **SKOS Simple Knowledge Organization System Reference.** W3C Recommendation, 18 August 2009. [Dated specification](https://www.w3.org/TR/2009/REC-skos-reference-20090818/).

Used for concepts, labels and knowledge-organization relations. A shared label or concept relation is not a proof of semantic equivalence or truth.

## Partial correctness

C. A. R. Hoare. **An Axiomatic Basis for Computer Programming.** *Communications of the ACM* 12(10), 1969, 576–580, 583. [DOI: 10.1145/363235.363259](https://doi.org/10.1145/363235.363259). [Author's institutional bibliography](https://www.cs.ox.ac.uk/people/publications/title/Tony.Hoare.html).

Used for the established idea of reasoning about programs through preconditions, postconditions and inference rules. The transformation paper adapts conditional preservation reasoning to its explicitly defined relations. Its composition argument is not presented as a novel replacement for program logic or a proof of termination.

## Submodular maximization

George L. Nemhauser, Laurence A. Wolsey and Marshall L. Fisher. **An Analysis of Approximations for Maximizing Submodular Set Functions—I.** *Mathematical Programming* 14, 1978, 265–294. [DOI: 10.1007/BF01588971](https://doi.org/10.1007/BF01588971).

Used for the classical greedy approximation result under normalized, monotone submodular value and a cardinality limit. The context-composition paper states those assumptions and explains why complementary knowledge units can violate submodularity. The guarantee is not transferred to arbitrary budgets, penalties or semantic quality functions.

## Retrieval-augmented generation

Patrick Lewis, Ethan Perez, Aleksandra Piktus, Fabio Petroni, Vladimir Karpukhin, Naman Goyal, Heinrich Küttler, Mike Lewis, Wen-tau Yih, Tim Rocktäschel, Sebastian Riedel and Douwe Kiela. **Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Knowledge-Intensive NLP Tasks.** *Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems* 33, 2020. [Conference publication](https://proceedings.neurips.cc/paper/2020/hash/6b493230205f780e1bc26945df7481e5-Abstract.html). [Author manuscript](https://arxiv.org/abs/2005.11401).

Used as the primary reference for generation conditioned on retrieved material. The context-composition paper studies explicit coverage and preservation obligations; it does not claim to have replicated the cited experiments or surpassed their models.

## Computational complexity

Richard M. Karp. **Reducibility among Combinatorial Problems.** In *Complexity of Computer Computations*, 1972, 85–103. [DOI: 10.1007/978-1-4684-2001-2_9](https://doi.org/10.1007/978-1-4684-2001-2_9).

Used as the primary source for the classical complexity context of set cover. The current paper gives its own explicit mapping from a finite covering problem to a restricted context-selection instance. This does not imply that every practical instance is hard or that finite exact search is impossible.

## Program slicing

Mark Weiser. **Program Slicing.** *IEEE Transactions on Software Engineering* SE-10(4), 1984, 352–357. [Original article](https://faculty.cc.gatech.edu/~harrold/6340/cs6340_fall2009/Readings/Weiser.Slicing.TSE.pdf).

Used in [semantic objects and context](../../papers/semantic-objects-and-context/PAPER.md) to identify the established behavior-preservation problem behind a backward slice. The paper gives a restricted acyclic model; it does not introduce a new general slicing algorithm or infer semantic preservation for arbitrary text.

## Truth maintenance

Jon Doyle. **A Truth Maintenance System.** *Artificial Intelligence* 12(3), 1979, 231–272. [Publisher record](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/0004370279900080). [MIT report version, AI Memo 521](https://dspace.mit.edu/entities/publication/5377b306-4ecc-4687-b1f5-78cbb4a0543a).

Used in [revision and governance](../../papers/revision-and-governance/PAPER.md) as prior work on recording reasons and revising beliefs as assumptions change. The project's reachability argument identifies potentially affected assessments; it is not an implementation of Doyle's system.

## Build systems

Andrey Mokhov, Neil Mitchell and Simon Peyton Jones. **Build Systems à la Carte.** *Proceedings of the ACM on Programming Languages* 2(ICFP), article 79, 2018, 1–29. [DOI: 10.1145/3236774](https://doi.org/10.1145/3236774). [Author's article](https://simon.peytonjones.org/assets/pdfs/build-systems-original.pdf).

Used in [controlled knowledge reuse](../../papers/controlled-knowledge-reuse/PAPER.md) for the established distinction between scheduling and rebuilding, and for correctness relative to declared dependencies. Agreement with build dependencies does not establish a source's truth, a new task's semantic requirements or current authority.

## Views and complements

François Bancilhon and Nicolas Spyratos. **Update Semantics of Relational Views.** *ACM Transactions on Database Systems* 6(4), 1981, 557–575. [Original article](https://cs.uwaterloo.ca/~david/cs848s18/constant-complement.pdf).

Used in [constrained transformations](../../papers/constrained-transformations/PAPER.md) as a prior construction in which a view and retained complement determine database state and constrain update translation. The analogy motivates retaining residual information; the project does not claim those view-update laws for natural-language projections.

## Theory change

Carlos E. Alchourrón, Peter Gärdenfors and David Makinson. **On the Logic of Theory Change: Partial Meet Contraction and Revision Functions.** *The Journal of Symbolic Logic* 50(2), 1985, 510–530. [DOI: 10.2307/2274239](https://doi.org/10.2307/2274239). [Publisher record](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/journal-of-symbolic-logic/article/abs/on-the-logic-of-theory-change-partial-meet-contraction-and-revision-functions/7ED837BAD5FB6D9A7C77906D73527F9C).

Used in [revision and governance](../../papers/revision-and-governance/PAPER.md) to distinguish logical contraction and revision from versioned artifacts and adoption decisions. The current model neither defines an AGM revision operator nor claims to satisfy the AGM postulates.

## Causal inference

Judea Pearl. **Causal Inference in Statistics: An Overview.** *Statistics Surveys* 3, 2009, 96–146. [DOI: 10.1214/09-SS057](https://doi.org/10.1214/09-SS057). [Author's institutional copy](https://escholarship.org/uc/item/1sv6k47m).

Used for the distinction between observed association and causal conclusions under assumptions about interventions and confounding. The utility paper therefore distinguishes measured task scores from identified causal benefits and does not treat a small exploratory comparison as a population estimate.

## HTTP caching

Roy T. Fielding, Mark Nottingham and Julian Reschke, editors. **HTTP Caching.** RFC 9111, Internet Standard, June 2022. [RFC 9111](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc9111.html).

Used as a reference for HTTP cache controls and freshness concepts. Knowledge applicability, semantic dependency closure and action authorization remain additional project obligations.

## Stale HTTP responses

Mark Nottingham. **HTTP Cache-Control Extensions for Stale Content.** RFC 5861, Informational, May 2010. [RFC 5861](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc5861.html).

Used as a clearly limited analogy for stale-while-revalidate and stale-if-error behavior. This document is informational, and its HTTP mechanisms do not authorize reuse of revoked knowledge or actions.

## Requirement language

Scott Bradner. **Key Words for Use in RFCs to Indicate Requirement Levels.** RFC 2119, BCP 14, March 1997. [RFC 2119](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc2119.html).

Barry Leiba. **Ambiguity of Uppercase vs Lowercase in RFC 2119 Key Words.** RFC 8174, BCP 14, May 2017. [RFC 8174](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8174.html).

Engineering specifications use capitalized MUST, MUST NOT, SHOULD, SHOULD NOT and MAY in this sense. Their force concerns conformance to the stated project specification; it is not a claim that the specification is an IETF standard.

## JSON exchange and schemas

Tim Bray, editor. **The JavaScript Object Notation (JSON) Data Interchange Format.** RFC 8259, Internet Standard, December 2017. [RFC 8259](https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8259).

Austin Wright, Henry Andrews, Ben Hutton and Greg Dennis, editors. **JSON Schema: A Media Type for Describing JSON Documents.** Draft 2020-12. [Official core specification](https://json-schema.org/draft/2020-12/json-schema-core).

Used for the exchange syntax and JSON Schema dialect of the bounded observation, dependency-change, book-package and index-receipt profiles. A schema validates the declared data shape and constraints; it does not authenticate an observation or perform every cross-record semantic check. The exact-byte hash profile does not imply JSON value canonicalization.

## Agent artifact formats

Agent Skills maintainers. **Agent Skills Specification.** [Official specification](https://agentskills.io/specification), consulted 4 November 2026.

Used for skill-directory naming, the required `SKILL.md` file, its `name` and `description` metadata, and supported extension metadata. The project defines its own additional reading structure while preserving compatibility with that format.

AGENTS.md maintainers. **AGENTS.md.** [Official format description](https://agents.md/), consulted 4 November 2026.

Used for repository instructions to coding agents. The format is ordinary Markdown and does not prescribe a universal schema for agent identities, competencies, roles or blueprints. The project's formats for those artifacts are explicitly its own conventions.

## Evaluation methods

The following sources explain methodological distinctions used in the studies. They do not authenticate the historical runs, supply missing fixtures or turn same-session reviews into independent replication.

Brian A. Nosek, Charles R. Ebersole, Alexander C. DeHaven and David T. Mellor. **The Preregistration Revolution.** *Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences* 115(11), 2018, 2600–2606. [DOI: 10.1073/pnas.1708274114](https://doi.org/10.1073/pnas.1708274114). [Primary article](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5856500/). The [editorial pilot](../../engineering/studies/editorial-skill-pilot/STUDY.md) distinguishes a protocol fixed before its retained outputs from an analysis composed after results were known. Its local recorded protocol was not registered in an external public registry.

Lianmin Zheng, Wei-Lin Chiang, Ying Sheng, Siyuan Zhuang, Zhanghao Wu, Yonghao Zhuang, Zi Lin, Zhuohan Li, Dacheng Li, Eric P. Xing, Hao Zhang, Joseph E. Gonzalez and Ion Stoica. **Judging LLM-as-a-Judge with MT-Bench and Chatbot Arena.** *Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 36, Datasets and Benchmarks Track*, 2023. [Author manuscript](https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.05685). Used for the need to examine judge biases and the limits of agreement. The editorial and [projection](../../engineering/studies/executable-projections-and-revision/STUDY.md) studies use their own coarse rubrics and masking; they do not inherit the cited benchmark's agreement or validation results.

Richard A. DeMillo, Richard J. Lipton and Frederick G. Sayward. **Hints on Test Data Selection: Help for the Practicing Programmer.** *Computer* 11(4), April 1978, 34–41. [Publisher record](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/abstract/document/1646911). [Institutional bibliographic record](https://www.cerias.purdue.edu/apps/reports_and_papers/view/919). This is historical background for deliberate program mutation. The project reports its declared mutants and whether they are distinguished; a finite mutation result is not general test adequacy.

Alfred Tarski. **A Lattice-Theoretical Fixpoint Theorem and Its Applications.** *Pacific Journal of Mathematics* 5(2), 1955, 285–309. [Publisher's original article](https://msp.org/pjm/1955/5-2/pjm-v5-n2-p11-s.pdf). This supplies general fixed-point background. The [dependency study](../../engineering/studies/dependency-closure-and-package-delivery/STUDY.md) computes the least closure by finite monotone iteration on the power set of a declared graph; its proof and implementation checks do not establish that every semantic dependency was recorded.

National Institute of Standards and Technology. **SCLITE Scoring Package: Alignment and Scoring Documentation.** [Official SCTK documentation](https://github.com/usnistgov/SCTK/blob/master/doc/sclite.htm), consulted 4 November 2026. Used for reference–hypothesis alignment and substitution, deletion and insertion counts underlying word-error reporting. The [transcription study](../../engineering/studies/source-transcription-and-uncertainty/STUDY.md) states its character- and word-unit definitions explicitly; it reports no measured CER or WER because the required reference and protocol are unavailable.

## Experimental source books

These books identify the historical materials used by the engineering studies. They are not sources for a new scientific theorem and their practical advice is not endorsed by this publication. The retained export identities, rather than the catalogue pages alone, identify the actual inputs that were processed.

- **Книга о вкусной и здоровой пище** (*The Book of Tasty and Healthy Food*). Ministry of Food Industry of the USSR. Moscow: Pishchepromizdat, 1952. [National Electronic Library record](https://rusneb.ru/catalog/000199_000009_005811805/). Used as the cooking export in the [transcription study](../../engineering/studies/source-transcription-and-uncertainty/STUDY.md). The retained export key `molchanova` and Russian label “Молчанова” identify that input in project reports; they are not a claim of sole authorship. The cited catalogue identifies the Ministry, and this edition does not infer an editor's identity from an export key.
- **Навигация, лоция и мореходная астрономия** (*Navigation, Pilotage, and Nautical Astronomy*). G. I. Fain. Moscow: Transport, 1977. [National Electronic Library record](https://rusneb.ru/catalog/000199_000009_007594442/). Used as the navigation export; the [retained corpus audit](../../engineering/studies/source-transcription-and-uncertainty/evidence/corpus-audit-source.json) identifies the processed representation.
- **Сто схемных задач по автоматике** (*One Hundred Circuit Problems in Automation*). V. A. Timofeev. Leningrad: Sudostroenie, 1964. These are the bibliographic descriptors recorded for the automation export in the [retained corpus audit](../../engineering/studies/source-transcription-and-uncertainty/evidence/corpus-audit-source.json). An independently verified stable catalogue record for this exact edition was not established in this review; the project audit is input provenance, not external bibliographic confirmation. The historical engineering tasks examine representations and preservation, not modern operational certification.

### Editorial input

Maxim Ilyakhov and Lyudmila Sarycheva. **Пиши, сокращай: Как создавать сильный текст** (*Write and Shorten: How to Create Strong Text*). 4th ed. Moscow: Alpina Publisher, 2024, 400 printed pages, **as declared in the supplied OCR bibliographic line**. [Publisher's work listing](https://alpinabook.ru/catalog/book-pishi-sokrashchay-2025/). No independently verified ISBN for the supplied edition is asserted.

The [editorial pilot](../../engineering/studies/editorial-skill-pilot/STUDY.md) used the project's skills derived from a supplied OCR representation of this work. The same derived package is examined in the [dependency-closure study](../../engineering/studies/dependency-closure-and-package-delivery/STUDY.md), whose [layout preservation report](../../engineering/studies/dependency-closure-and-package-delivery/evidence/layout-preservation-report.json) and [dependency graph](../../engineering/studies/dependency-closure-and-package-delivery/evidence/dependency-graph.json) retain the source's record names and dependency metadata.

The supplied filename uses “2025,” and the digital input contains 427 physical page entries. These descriptors differ from the OCR bibliographic line and do not authenticate an edition. The publisher's current listing identifies the work but does not authenticate the exact privately supplied file. The publication distributes the pilot's own texts, evaluations and package metadata, not the book or the private source-derived toolkit.

## Project sources and evidence

Pavel Valentov. **Knowledge Contract and Meaning Management: earlier publication corpus.** Repository snapshot [b50243b28e9ad7376e28c749a21479ef46ec9d60](https://github.com/Arcanada-one/knowledge-contract/tree/b50243b28e9ad7376e28c749a21479ef46ec9d60).

The [source map](../../appendices/source-map/APPENDIX.md) records the migration from fourteen earlier paper files and ten specifications to the current reading structure. The [engineering evidence index](../../engineering/studies/evidence-index.json) binds individual empirical or analytical claims to their supporting records. Each study describes what can be rerun publicly, what is available only as a retained report, and what remains unmeasured.
